Résumé de la thèse Le site de production des eaux de Vittel® fait figure de pionnier dans la prise en compte des enjeux environnementaux dans la production d’eau en bouteille. Afin de préserver la qualité des ressources en eau, l’entreprise Nestlé Waters a développé un partenariat avec l’Institut National de Recherche pour l’Agriculture, l’Alimentation et l’Environnement (INRAE). Les actions entreprises ont des effets bénéfiques sur la biodiversité, les paysages, affectant ainsi le bien-être des populations. Le lien entre le bien-être humain et la qualité de son environnement est formalisé à travers le concept de services écosystémiques (SE). [SOURCE: Page_6]

Ces SE sont parfois mal pris en compte dans les décisions économiques, du fait qu’ils ne soient pas échangés directement sur un marché. La science économique a développé un panel de méthodes d’évaluation dont l’objet est de leur assigner une valeur monétaire. Cela permet de les intégrer à la prise de décision, notamment à travers la mise en place de Paiements pour Services Environnementaux (PSE). [SOURCE: Page_6]

La notion de paiement pourrait nous faire penser que l'évaluation monétaire a un rôle à jouer pour établir la bonne fourniture de services. Mais, habituellement, les paiements sont rarement alignés sur la valeur des services environnementaux, et plutôt établis par négociation. Mais l'évaluation monétaire peut également être utilisée pour évaluer les co-bénéfices de ces programmes. Ces co-bénéfices peuvent représenter une part substantielle des bénéfices totaux. Ne pas les prendre en compte peut conduire à une sous-estimation importante des effets d'une politique. [SOURCE: Page_6]

L'objectif de ce projet de thèse est d'étudier les co-bénéfices du PSE de Vittel. Dans le premier chapitre, j'ai mené un Dicrete Choice Experiment (DCE) pour étudier les préférences de la population locale autour de Vittel : l'idée était de tester différents scénarios d'activités récréatives. Chaque scénario était notamment composé d'attributs liés aux mesures agro- environnementales mises en œuvre sur le bassin versant de Vittel. Les personnes interrogées expriment des préférences pour certaines caractéristiques environnementales, comme la présence d'un cours d'eau ou la non-utilisation de pesticides, qui est une mesure importante du PSE. Dans le chapitre deux, j'ai mis en œuvre un autre DCE qui visait, cette fois, à cibler les non-utilisateurs des ressources en eau. Les résultats ont montré que les préférences pour les attributs environnementaux étaient très hétérogènes. J'ai estimé un modèle à classes latentes avec quatre classes. Les individus des classes 1 et 3 sont prêts à payer un montant important sur la facture d'eau pour interdire les pesticides. Les répondants de la deuxième [SOURCE: Page_6]

4 [SOURCE: Page_6]

classe ont un CAP élevé pour certaines mesures environnementales (interdiction des pesticides, restauration des cours d'eau etc.). Enfin, les personnes regroupées dans la quatrième classe n'avaient que des préférences pour l'interdiction des pesticides et la restauration des cours d'eau. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Dans le troisième chapitre, j'ai réalisé une analyse hédonique sur le département des Vosges. L'objectif était d'évaluer l'influence des différentes utilisations du sol sur les prix des logements. J'ai défini cinq catégories : agriculture, forêts, prairies, zones urbaines et « autres ». Les résultats montrent que les prairies influencent de manière significative les prix des logements, mais sont moins significatifs pour les maisons situées à l'intérieur de l'impluvium de Vittel. Néanmoins, un effet positif de l'agriculture est observé. [SOURCE: Page_7]

Enfin, le dernier chapitre fournit un article relatif aux enquêtes réalisées dans le cadre de la thèse : les deux DCE et une troisième enquête relative aux préférences des touristes en matière de gestion des paysages forestiers et agricoles dans la région de Vittel. Cette dernière enquête complète ce qui a été fait dans les chapitres 1 et 2 pour les populations locales et non-locales. [SOURCE: Page_7]

5 [SOURCE: Page_7]

Summary of the thesis The Vittel® water production site is a pioneer in taking environmental issues into account in the production of bottled water. In order to preserve the quality of water resources, the company Nestlé Waters has developed a partnership with INRAE. The actions undertaken have beneficial effects on biodiversity and landscapes, thus affecting the well-being of populations. The link between human well-being and the quality of its environment is formalized through the concept of ecosystem services (ES). [SOURCE: Page_8]

These ES are sometimes poorly taken into account in economic decisions, due to the fact that they are not exchanged directly on a market. Researchers in economics have developed a range of evaluation methods whose purpose is to assign them a monetary value. This makes it possibleto integrate them into decision-making, in particular through the implementation of Payments for Environmental Services (PES). [SOURCE: Page_8]

The notion of payment could make us think that monetary evaluation has a role to play in establishing the correct provision of services. But, typically, payments are rarely aligned with the value of environmental services, and rather established through negotiation. Monetary valuation can also be used to assess the co-benefits of these programs. These co-benefits can represent a substantial part of the total benefits. Not taking them into account can lead to a significant underestimation of the effects of a policy. [SOURCE: Page_8]

The objective of this thesis project is to study the co-benefits of Vittel's PES. In the first chapter, I conducted a Discrete Choice Experiment (DCE) to study the preferences of the local population around Vittel: the idea was to test different scenarios of recreational activities. Each scenario was notably composed of attributes linked to the agro-environmental measures implemented on the Vittel watershed. Respondents expressed preferences for certain environmental features, such as the presence of a watercourse or the non-use of pesticides, which is an important measure of the PES. In chapter two, I implemented another DCE, which aimed, this time, at targeting non-users of water resources. The results showed that preferences for environmental attributes were very heterogeneous. I estimated a latent class model with four classes. Individuals in classes 1 and 3 were willing to pay a significant amount on the water bill to ban pesticides. Respondents in the second class had a high WTP for certain environmental measures (ban on pesticides, restoration of waterways, etc.). Finally, people grouped in the fourth class only had positive preferences for banning pesticides and restoring waterways. [SOURCE: Page_8]

6 [SOURCE: Page_8]

In the third chapter, I carried out a hedonic analysis on the Vosges department. The objective was to assess the influence of different land uses on housing prices. I defined five categories: agriculture, forests, grasslands, urban areas and “others”. The results show that meadows significantly influence housing prices, but are less significant for houses located inside the Vittel impluvium. Nevertheless, a positive effect of agriculture is observed. Finally, the last chapter provides an article relating to the surveys carried out as part of the thesis: the two DCEs and a third survey related to the preferences of tourists in terms of management of forest and agricultural landscapes in the Vittel region. This last survey completes what has been done in chapters 1 and 2 for the local and non-local populations. [SOURCE: Page_9]

7 [SOURCE: Page_9]

Résumé long de la thèse [SOURCE: Page_10]

Le site de production des eaux de Vittel® fait figure de pionnier dans la prise en compte des enjeux environnementaux dans la production d’eau en bouteille. Afin de préserver la qualité des ressources en eau, l’entreprise Nestlé Waters a développé un partenariat avec l’Institut National de Recherche pour l’Agriculture, l’Alimentation et l’Environnement (INRAE). La question (générale) de recherche était alors la suivante: “quels sont les changements à mettre en oeuvre dans l'activité agricole du site de l’impluvium et dans quelles conditions les réaliser, pour réduire le taux des nitrates sous les racines des plantes cultivées et des prairies et pour le maintenir durablement sous le seuil des 10 mg par litre?”. S’engage alors, en 1989, le programme de recherche Agriculture Environnement Vittel (AGREV), dont la première phase, AGREV 1 aboutit à la rédaction d’un cahier des charges^1 qui définit un certain nombre de nouvelles pratiques, comme par exemple, la suppression de la culture du maïs ou le compostage de l’ensemble des déjections animales. Ce cahier des charges s’inscrit dans le cadre d’un arrangement contractuel (qui prend la forme d’une convention). Le contrat vise alors à internaliser une externalité négative (la pollution de l’eau) en modifiant l’activité principale de production (ici agricole). Internaliser les externalités revient alors à faire intégrer au pollueur le coût de cette pollution dans son raisonnement économique, notamment via ses coûts de production et in fine, via le prix de ses produits. Le service environnemental apparaît alors comme un outil permettant de limiter les externalités négatives en les internalisant. Les services environnementaux sont produits de manière intentionnelle et leur coût de production n’est pas nul pour celui qui le fournit. Il faut donc inciter les producteurs potentiels de services à les produire en les rémunérant. Les actions entreprises ont des effets bénéfiques sur la biodiversité, les paysages etc. affectant ainsi le bien-être des populations. Le lien entre le bien- être humain et la qualité de son environnement est formalisé à travers le concept de services écosystémiques (SE). [SOURCE: Page_10]

Les rapports sur l’état des écosystèmes se sont succédé ces dernières années: tous partagent le constat d’une détérioration des écosystèmes, ainsi que d’un déclin de la biodiversité. Ces rapports pointent également le risque de dépassement d’un seuil, appelé « seuil critique », au- delà duquel la capacité de ces écosystèmes à fournir des services contribuant au bien-être humain serait compromise et ce, alors même que la participation des écosystèmes au bien- être humain est aujourd’hui largement documentée dans la littérature scientifique. [SOURCE: Page_10]

Ces SE sont parfois mal pris en compte dans les décisions économiques, du fait qu’ils ne [SOURCE: Page_10]

8 [SOURCE: Page_10]

soient pas échangés directement sur un marché. La science économique a développé un panel de méthodes d’évaluation dont l’objet est de leur assigner une valeur monétaire. Cette valeur est définie en termes de compromis que les individus sont prêts à accepter entre différentes situations. La valeur d'un bien ou d'un service s’apprécie donc à partir de la quantité maximale d'un autre bien (ou d’une somme d’argent) qu'un individu serait prêt à abandonner ou recevoir en échange d'une amélioration (ou d’une dégradation) du bien ou service considéré. La valeur économique totale (VET) d'un bien environnemental peut alors être décomposée selon une classification standard. Cette classification montre que la VET d'un bien environnemental inclut des valeurs dites “d’usage” et des valeurs dites de “non-usage”. Les valeurs d’usage incluent les valeurs d’usage direct du bien environnemental (usage de consommation commerciale ou non, ou d’utilisation non destructrice du bien) et les valeurs d’usage indirect qui découlent notamment de ses fonctions écologiques. Les valeurs de non-usage font référence à la valeur que les individus accordent au bien même s'ils ne l'utilisent pas. Il s’agit des valeurs d’option, liées au bien-être retiré de la conservation d’une possibilité d’usage (ou non usage) futur, des valeurs de legs, liées au bien-être retiré de la transmission d’un bien environnemental aux générations futures et des valeurs d’existence liées au bien-être retiré de la préservation de la valeur intrinsèque de la nature, en dehors de toute considération humaine. [SOURCE: Page_11]

Assigner une valeur à ces services permet de les intégrer à la prise de décision, notamment à travers la mise en place de Paiements pour Services Environnementaux (PSE). Les PSE s’inscrivent dans le cadre du développement d’instruments économiques visant à préserver les écosystèmes: ces derniers constituent l’une des trois grandes familles d’outils de protection de l’environnement, avec les instruments réglementaires de types « command and control » et les instruments d’information. Les concepts de SE et les mécanismes de types PSE ne peuvent être dissociés. Cependant, l’origine des deux notions est distincte: alors que la notion de SE a une portée plutôt analytique, mettant en avant dans la littérature l’idée que les services rendus par la nature ne sont pas suffisamment pris en compte, voire ignorés; le concept de PSE, a, quant à lui une dimension plus opérationnelle, liée à l’efficacité des politiques de conservation dans les pays en développement. [SOURCE: Page_11]

La notion de paiement pourrait nous faire penser que l'évaluation monétaire a un rôle à jouer pour assurer la bonne fourniture de services. Mais, habituellement, les paiements sont rarement alignés sur la valeur des services environnementaux, et plutôt établis par négociation. Mais l'évaluation monétaire peut également être utilisée pour évaluer les co- bénéfices de ces programmes. Ces co-bénéfices peuvent représenter une part substantielle des bénéfices totaux. Ne pas les prendre en compte peut conduire à une sous-estimation 9 [SOURCE: Page_11]

importante des effets d'une politique. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Pour estimer les valeurs liées aux SE, différentes méthodes d'évaluation sont disponibles, souvent classées en deux catégories : les méthodes basées sur les préférences révélées et les méthodes basées sur les préférences déclarées. Les premières consistent à estimer des valeurs non marchandes en observant un comportement réel qui est lié d'une manière ou d'une autre à un bien ou à un attribut environnemental, tandis que les secondes consistent à poser directement aux individus des questions liées à leurs préférences dans le cadre d'une enquête et à déduire des valeurs à partir de leurs réponses déclarées. Dans ce manuscrit, j’utilise à la fois des méthodes de préférences déclarées (Discrete Choice Experiment (DCE) et binary choice question) et révélées (méthode des prix hédoniques). Le DCE appartient à la famille des méthodes des préférences déclarées. Cette méthode a, par le passé, été utilisée pour l'évaluation d’un large ensemble de SE ou pour examiner le choix des agriculteurs d'adopter des pratiques respectueuses de l'environnement. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Dans le premier chapitre, j'ai mené un Dicrete Choice Experiment (DCE) pour étudier les préférences de la population locale autour de Vittel : l'idée était de tester différents scénarios d'activités récréatives. Chaque scénario était notamment composé d'attributs liés aux mesures agro-environnementales mises en œuvre sur le bassin versant de Vittel (absence de pesticides, présence de haies etc.). Compte tenu du fait que l'objectif était de sonder la population locale autour de l’impluvium de Vittel (et de fait des deux municipalités de Vittel et Contrexéville), un périmètre d’un rayon d’environ 15 kilomètres autour de cette zone a été établi, correspondant au bassin de vie de Vittel. Dans ce périmètre, les municipalités (sauf les plus grandes Vittel, Contrexéville et Mirecourt) ont été sélectionnées aléatoirement. L’enquête se divise en trois parties: 1. un recueil de données sur les individus enquêtés: caractéristiques sociodémographiques (âge, diplôme, professions et catégories socioprofessionnelles (PCS), etc.) et attitudes (sensibilité aux enjeux environnementaux, attachement au territoire, etc.) ; 2. des informations détaillées sur les sorties récréatives: fréquence, distance, mode de déplacement, lieu identifié précisément sur carte interactive, etc. ; 3. Une expérimentation par les choix sur les activités récréatives (loisirs) de la population locale (promenade, cueillette, chasse, etc.) et ses préférences vis-à-vis de certaines caractéristiques environnementales et sociales (paysage, biodiversité, etc.) Les personnes interrogées expriment des préférences pour certaines caractéristiques environnementales, comme la présence d'un cours d'eau ou la non-utilisation de pesticides, qui est une mesure importante du PSE. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Dans le chapitre deux, j'ai mis en œuvre un autre DCE qui visait, cette fois, à cibler les non- [SOURCE: Page_12]

10 [SOURCE: Page_12]

utilisateurs des ressources en eau. J’ai inclus les répondants qui vivent à différentes distances de la zone du projet. Les répondants qui vivent loin de la zone du projet ne sont pas censés avoir une valeur d'usage direct mais seulement une valeur de non-usage du projet. Cependant, il a également été démontré dans la littérature qu'il peut exister un effet de la distance sur les valeurs de non-usage. Même si les services environnementaux et sociaux ne profitent pas directement aux individus, ils préfèrent que ces services soient rendus proches de leur lieu de vie. Par conséquent, nous considérerons dans notre analyse les locaux (usagers potentiels) et les non-locaux (non-usagers) vivant à différentes distances de la zone du projet. L’hypothèse à tester est que le CAP des enquêtés diminue à mesure que la distance qui les sépare du bien ou du site auquel ils accordent de la valeur diminue. Les résultats ont montré que les préférences pour les attributs environnementaux étaient très hétérogènes. J'ai estimé un modèle à classes latentes avec quatre classes. Les individus des classes 1 et 3 sont prêts à payer un montant important sur la facture d'eau pour interdire les pesticides. Les répondants de la deuxième classe ont un CAP élevé pour certaines mesures environnementales (interdiction des pesticides, restauration des cours d'eau etc.). Enfin, les personnes regroupées dans la quatrième classe n'avaient que des préférences pour l'interdiction des pesticides et la restauration des cours d'eau. Nous avons également étudié un éventuel effet de la distance sur les préférences des non-utilisateurs en matière d'attributs environnementaux. Cependant, les résultats sont modérés et semblent indiquer que la distance n'a d'importance que pour une petite partie des personnes interrogées (réparties dans la classe 3). [SOURCE: Page_13]

Dans le troisième chapitre, j'ai réalisé une analyse hédonique sur le département des Vosges en utilisant, d’une part les données de la base Perval sur les transactions immobilières, entre 1998 et 2021 (Notaires de France). L'objectif était d'évaluer l'influence des différents usages du sol (en utilisant les données Corine Land Cover pour les années 1990, 2000, 2006, 2012 et 2018) sur les prix des logements (10 801 observations après nettoyage des données). J'ai défini cinq catégories : agriculture, forêts, prairies, zones urbaines et « autres ». Différentes spécifications sont utilisées: d’une part la distance entre les logements et les différents usages du sol; d’autre part la part de chaque usage du sol dans des « zones-tampons » plus ou moins larges (250m, 500m et 1km). Un des objectifs était de vérifier l’hypothèse selon laquelle il pourrait exister un effet de ces usages du sol dans l’impluvium et de comparer avec l’ensemble du département des Vosges. Les résultats montrent, entre autres, que les prairies influencent de manière significative les prix des logements, mais sont moins significatifs pour les maisons situées à l'intérieur de l'impluvium de Vittel. Néanmoins, un effet positif de l'agriculture est observé. Ce chapitre permet de compléter les résultats des deux premiers chapitres (basés sur [SOURCE: Page_13]

11 [SOURCE: Page_13]

une méthode de préférences déclarées) à une échelle différente et avec une méthode de préférences révélées. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Enfin, le dernier chapitre fournit un article relatif aux enquêtes réalisées dans le cadre de la thèse : les deux DCE et une troisième enquête relative aux préférences des touristes (ou excursionnistes) en matière de gestion des paysages forestiers et agricoles dans la région de Vittel. Cette dernière enquête complète ce qui a été fait dans les chapitres 1 et 2 pour les populations locales et non-locales. Elle a été notamment été menée via les lieux touristiques de Vittel et de sa région (comme les offices de tourisme). Elle fournit des informations sur le profil des visiteurs, les raisons de leur visite, les dépenses effectuées dans les commerces, leur conaissance des actions menées par Agrivair sur l’impluvium, et enfin, sur leurs Consentements à Payer (CAP) pour que les actions entreprises continuent d’être menées. Cette enquête répond à l’autre enjeu essentiel de la thèse, à savoir mesurer l’impact sur l’économie locale (commerces, tourisme etc.) des mesures de préservations de la qualité de l’eau et de la biodiversité prises sur l’impluvium. Les bases de données issues de ces enquêtes constituant un des principaux apports de la thèse, elles sont stockées dans un entrepôt de données (https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/dataverse.xhtml?alias=PHD-BETA) et accessibles pour des travaux ultérieurs. [SOURCE: Page_14]

L'objectif de ce projet de thèse était d'étudier les co-bénéfices du PSE de Vittel, i.e. les bénéfices qui s'ajoutent à la qualité de l'eau. Ce travail s’appuie sur notamment sur différentes enquêtes, réalisées à des échelles variées (autour de l’impluvium de Vittel, mais aussi à une échelle plus large) et auprès de différents publics (population locale autour de l’impluvium de Vittel, touristes de la région etc.). Les résultats montrent que ces différentes populations sont prêtes à payer, à des degrés divers, pour bénéficier des retombées environementales et sociales des mesures prises sur l’impluvium de Vittel. La thèse montre aussi un effet positif sur l’économie locale, notamment via l’activité touristique. [SOURCE: Page_14]

Pour autant, la thèse ne fournit pas d'Analyse Coût-Bénéfices (ACB) du PSE de Vittel. Étant donné que les PSE ont commencé à être mis en œuvre dans les années 1980, cette thèse fournit une sorte de processus d'évaluation ex post d'un programme de PSE particulier. L'évaluation des avantages et des coûts potentiels devrait néanmoins être réalisée ex ante afin de prendre des décisions plus éclairées. C'est pourquoi nous pouvons considérer ce travail comme un premier pas vers une évaluation économique plus systématique des programmes de PSE, afin de fournir aux décideurs des outils leur permettant de décider s'ils constituent le meilleur instrument pour la préservation de l'environnement. [SOURCE: Page_14]

12 [SOURCE: Page_14]

Abstracts and keywords of the chapters [SOURCE: Page_15]

Chapitre 1: The Impact of Water Protection Measures in the Vittel Impluvium on Recreational Values: A Choice Experiment with Local Residents [SOURCE: Page_15]

Abstract Because agriculture jointly provides multiple ecosystem services (ES), actions undertaken for the protection of spring water catchment areas by mineral water companies may produce positive externalities. By implementing environmentally friendly measures in contracting with farmers and forest managers, private water companies do not only create value for themselves and their consumers, but also contribute to the local population welfare with increased level of biodiversity and ES (e.g., recreation and tourism, aesthetic and scenic beauty, cultural heritage) as co-benefits of the protection of water quality. This paper aims at assessing the environmental and social preferences of the local population of Vittel (France) and surroundings, the area where Nestlé Waters produces the natural mineral waters of Vittel®, Contrex® and Hépar®, applying a discrete choice experiment (DCE). We considered “common” recreational areas (i.e., countryside and forest) along with relevant attributes identified in previous studies (e.g., facilities such as picnic tables or information signs, hiking and biking paths) and attributes based on specific measures (e.g., no use of pesticides, presence of hedgerows) implemented by Agrivair, a subsidiary of Nestlé Waters dedicated to the ecological issues in the impluvium. We found that people would prefer to avoid areas with pesticides and even those where they do not know if pesticides are used, for their recreational activities. The preference for hedgerows is shown to be low, sometimes negative, highlighting potential trade-off between biodiversity and recreation activities. [SOURCE: Page_15]

Keywords: Mineral Water Company; Vittel; Water quality protection; Environmental and recreational co-benefits; Discrete Choice Experiment. [SOURCE: Page_15]

13 [SOURCE: Page_15]

Chapitre 2: The non-use value of improving water quality and biodiversity: Preferences for watershed protection measures [SOURCE: Page_16]

Abstract This paper measures the non-use values of water and biodiversity protection in a watershed in eastern France. It is generally assumed that measures to protect water quality generate direct use values for water consumers, and that the use and non-use values of biodiversity improvement are difficult to disentangle. We conducted a discrete choice experiment with a non-local population to estimate the willingness to pay for restrictions on pesticide and fertilizer use on agricultural land, hedgerow protection and planting, watercourse restoration and sustainable forest management. We first estimate a mixed logit, which shows significant positive preferences for measures that have positive impacts on water quality and biodiversity, but with strong heterogeneity in preferences for the different environmental attributes. An in- depth latent class analysis shows more moderate results. We find that the monetary attribute has a small significant effect for the majority of respondents. While individuals show strong opposition to the status quo and clear preferences for environmental attributes, their marginal WTP is only significantly different from zero for the pesticide ban. While we identify a group of people who are strongly opposed to the environmental project, they are still willing to pay for a pesticide ban, as is the rest of the survey sample. [SOURCE: Page_16]

Keywords: Environmental co-benefits, non-use values (NUVs), distance decay, mixed logit, latent class model. [SOURCE: Page_16]

14 [SOURCE: Page_16]

Chapitre 3: Do payment for environmental services (PES) schemes increase housing prices in rural areas? hedonic pricing of environmental amenities [SOURCE: Page_17]

Abstract This paper investigates the influence of different land uses on housing prices in the Vosges department. We use the perval database and the corine land cover data to assess land use preferences around houses. Results for the Vosges department are compared to the impluvium area around the Vittel municipality, where a private compagny implements a payment for ecosystem services (PES) scheme to preserve water quality. This PES directly impacts the land use by developping grasslands area, or modifying agricultural landscapes. In this article, we tested boththe effect of distance to various land uses (forest, agriculture, grasslands etc.) as well a the landuse domination in some radius (250m, 500m, 1km) around houses. Using a Poisson model, weshow that grasslands have a positive effect on house prices in les Vosges. In addition, forest areas seem to have a positive influence on prices as well, but only when using the 1km buffer. That may suggests a potential appeal for forest as a recreational area. [SOURCE: Page_17]

Keywords : Hedonic analysis, payment for environmental services, environmental co-benefits [SOURCE: Page_17]

15 [SOURCE: Page_17]

Chapitre 4: Data production on environmental preferences for agri-environmental measures in the Vittel impluvium [SOURCE: Page_18]

Abstract Implementing measures to preserve the quality of spring water (controlling agricultural uses, restricting the use of pesticides, planting hedgerows, etc.) generates benefits beyond the water resource. Indeed, residents can benefit from landscapes and recreational sites shaped by these measures. Economic valuation techniques make it possible to quantify these co-benefits in monetary terms. Some of these techniques elicit preferences through surveys, such as the discrete choice experiment method (DCE). More globally, surveys are known to be useful in the nonmarket valuation field. This paper presents the data obtained from different survey exercises: two DCEs and a questionnaire distributed to tourists in the Vittel region. [SOURCE: Page_18]

Keywords: Data production, discrete choice experiment, environmental preferences [SOURCE: Page_18]

### 1. Ecosystem services : a concept between economics and ecology

Reports on the state of ecosystems have followed one another in recent years (Commissariat général au développement durable, 2020a; IPBES, 2019; TEEB, 2010), all sharing the observation that ecosystems are deteriorating and that biodiversity is declining. These reports also point at the risk of exceeding a « critical » threshold, beyond which the capacity of these ecosystems to provide services contributing to human well-being would be compromised (Commissariat général au développement durable, 2020a; TEEB, 2010; MEA 2005). However, the participation of ecosystems in human well-being is now widely documented in the scientific literature (Hernández-Blanco et al. 2022; Wang et al. 2021, Costanza et al. 1997, among others). However, many topics are still to explore, such as how to better integrate ecosystem valuation in decision-making (Marre et al. 2016; Laurans et al. 2013) or how to deal with the co-benefits associated with ecosystem management (Le Coent et al. 2023). [SOURCE: Page_26]

The ecosystem service (ES) concept links human well-being to the quality of the environment, connecting economics (« services ») and ecology (« ecosystems ») (Dufour et al. 2016). On the one hand, the term "ecosystem" has been introduced in the literature by the British ecologist A.G. Tansley (1935) after asking the botanist A.R Clapham about a « suitable word to denote the physical and biological components of an environment considered in relation to each other as a unit » (Willis, 1994). On the other hand, the vision of nature as a provider of service goes back at least to the XIXth century (Administration des eaux et forêts, 1808), even if the idea that humans benefit from nature dates back at least to Plato (cited in Serpentié et al. 2012 and Daily, 1997). In economics, these services are at the core of three different approaches, each one corresponding to different research fields: ecological economics, environmental economics, and services economics (Aznar, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_26]

a) In the ecological economics approach, environmental services are defined as services provided by nature to human, and not produced by human (Aznar, 2002). This is the definition defended in the seminal paper by Costanza et al. (1997). The consequence is that the intentionality of the service is irrelevent here, because humans are not at the origin of its provision (Aznar, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_26]

24 [SOURCE: Page_26]

b) Environmental economics is based on the notion of externalities. A lot of externalities’ definitions have been proposed since the apparition of the concept with Sidgwick (1887) and Marshall (1890) at the end of the XIXth century (Grolleau and Salhi, 2009). These definitions have in common the unintentional nature of externalities (Dorfman, 1993; Baumol et Oates, 1988; Catin, 1985; Hill, 1977). Cornes and Sandler (1996) even emphasize that this external effect is "often ignored by the one who generated it". As a consequence, it does not induce any additional production cost from the economic actor at the origin of the externality (Aznar; 2002) and is not subject to any market transaction (Froger et al. 2012; Picard; 1998 cited by Mollard, 2003). These market failures may ultimately lead to suboptimal situations (Spash; 2021). "Service-externality" (Aznar et al. 2009; Aznar 2002) or "internalization services" (Jeanneaux et al. 2011) are then seen as a way to internalize externalities, typically throught payments for environmental sevices (PES) (Hernandez and Benoit, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_27]

c) The services economics (Gadrey, 2000; Delaunay and Gadrey, 1987; Hill, 1977) approach distinguish clearly services from externalities, based on the notion of intentionality (Hill, 1977): externalities are « unsolicited » services. On the contrary, in order to talk about services, the provider must act intentionally and with the agreement of the beneficiary. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Interest of economists, on the one hand, and ecologists and biologists on the other hand, for each others’ disciplines is not new, resulting, for instance, in the development of bioeconomic models of fisheries in the 1950’s (Schaefer 1957; Scott 1955; Gordon, 1954). The idea of services provided by nature is explicitly mentioned in 1970’s^1 with different terms: "environmental services" (SCEP, 1970), "public-services functions of the ecosystem" (Holdren and Ehrlich, 1974), which are close to the notions "public services of the global ecosystem" (Ehrlich et al. 1977), "nature’s services" (Westman, 1977) and "ecosystem services" (Ehrlich and Ehrlich, 1981). Numerous publications trace back the origin of the notion of ES and its economic roots (Méral, 2016; Froger et al. 2015, 2012; Serpentié et al. 2012 among others). [SOURCE: Page_27]

### 2. From ecosystem services (ES) to payment for environmental services (PES)

Pesche et al. (2011) indicated that the emergence of ES and PES concepts are clearly distincts. Le Coq et al. (2016) date back the developement of the first PES initiatives to the mid-1990s. At this time, PES programs are seen as an alternative to existing tools for conservation, such [SOURCE: Page_27]

1 It followed early work from Marsh (1864), Osborn (1948), Vogt, (1948) or Leopold (1949). 2 Wunder adjusted its own definition in Wunder (2015). [SOURCE: Page_27]

25 [SOURCE: Page_27]

as Integrated Development and Conservation Projects (IDCP): IDCP were biodiversity conservation projects associated with rural development components (Huges and Flintan, 2001). IDCP worked as indirect incentives: the idea was to change the logic of local production by developping alternative livelihoods that represented a weaker risk for biodiversity, such as agroforestry, weaving or ecotourism (Huges and Flintan, 2001). However, they have been criticized for their complexity, the lack of awareness of local knowledge and practices or a poor objective definition (Brandon, 2001; Ferraro, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_28]

In opposition to indirect incentives, PES schemes appeared as a more efficient way to acheive conservation in the long term (Ferraro and Kiss, 2002; Ferraro, 2001). Three emblematic PES programs started to be implemented in different geographic areas: the Vittel watershed (Perrot-Maitre, 2006), New York (Heal and Chichilnisky, 1998) and the Costa Rica (Le Coq and Saenz Segura, 2016). These PES implementations are often presented as success stories (Laurans et al. 2011). The most well-known definition is probably Wunder’s one^2 (2005), but plenty of others exist (Wunder, 2015; Sattler and Matzdorf, 2013). [SOURCE: Page_28]

Wunder (2005) define a PES as « a voluntary transaction where a well-defined ES (or a landuse likely to secure that service) is being ‘bought’ by a (minimum one) ES buyer from a (minimum one) ES provider if and only if the ES provider secures ES provision (conditionality) ». The fact that the definition (and many others) suggests the idea of a payment could make us think that monetary valuation has a role to play to establish the right provision of services. But as Karsenty (2013) puts it « payments are not aligned with the (calculated) value of environmental services, but are established through negotiation and should, in theory, cover at least the opportunity cost of restrictions or changes in use (the net cost of foregoing an activity) ». For example, in the Vittel PES’ case, the activity (in the bottled water sector) of a private compagny, Nestlé Waters, was threatened by agricultural diffuse pollution due to intensive agricultural practices. Because of the diffuse character of pollution, individual responsabilities of farmers in water quality were difficult to establish: indeed, natural factors (precipitations, soil quality and so on) can affect the degree of pollution (Depres et al. 2008). In addition, all farms were not located at the same distance from the springs, which makes difficult to assess the role of each farm in pollution (Perrot-Maitre, 2006). The result of the bargaining process, described by Depres et al. (2008) and Perrot- Maitre (2006) confirmed the idea defended by Karsenty (2013): individual payments are not indexed to the improvement of water quality, but rather on the investments needed and the cost of adoption of new farming practices. [SOURCE: Page_28]

26 [SOURCE: Page_28]

### 3. The importance of co-benefits

Does this imply that nonmarket valuation is useless in the context of PES implementation ? Actually, monetary valuation can also be used to assess the co-benefits of such schemes i.e benefits which « accrue as a side effect of targeted policies » (Pearce, 2000). They are also known as secondary benefits, policy spillover effects or ancillary benefits (Pearce, 2000). Assessing these co-benefits is particularly important: they can represent a substantial part of total benefits. Not taking them into account could lead to an important underestimation of the total benefits of a policy (Hérivaux et Grémont, 2019) in cost-benefit analysis, for instance (Le Coent et al. 2023). Besides, the population enjoying these benefit is wider than just the local population (Hérivaux and Le Coent, 2021). Ignoring this can lead to underestimate the real number of beneficiaries as well. [SOURCE: Page_29]

### 4. Monetary valuation of ecosystem services: a brief history

As indicated above, the rise of the ecosystem services’ term and the use of monetary valuation methods is closely intertwined with the history of environmental and ecological economics. The rise of environmental economics is indissociable from the independent research organization Ressources For the Future (RFF). RFF was founded in 1952 in order to « support the conservation, development, and use of natural resources » (Wichman and Wietelman, 2019). RFF’s original research programs were organized around major groups of natural resources, including land, energy and minerals, and water resources (Wichman and Wietelman, 2019). But the history of environmental valuation methods is anterior to the development of RFF: in 1947, ten economists were contacted by the National Park Service (NPS) about a potential assessement of the benefits of national parks in the USA. Almost all of them discouraged such an attempt, because of the difficulty to measure these benefits, especially in monetary terms. However, one of them, Harold Hoteling, accepted the challenge, laying the foundations of the Travel Cost Method (TCM). The results of these investigations were presented by an economist of the land and recreational planning division, Roy A. Prewitt (1949): [SOURCE: Page_29]

« […] it might be better to forget the words “economic value of recreation” and focus attention on the expenditures induced (emphasis, in original). People spend money to be able to enjoy a certain experience (recreation) and there are economic effects arising from the impact of this expenditure upon the economy. It is on this area only that an objective approach can be made toward a solution or an answer to the question of recreational values ». [SOURCE: Page_29]

27 [SOURCE: Page_29]

R.A Prewitt (1949) p.19 [SOURCE: Page_30]

The NPS finally did not follow up Hoteling’s proposal (Hanemann, 2006; Pearce, 2002) but Hoteling efforts were finally rewarded: in 1956, the State of California hired an economic consulting company to estimate recreational benefits associated with a water-related project. This company decided to apply Hotelling’s idea: they implemented and conducted a survey, targeting lake visitors in the Sierras, collecting data on how far they had traveled and how much they had spent. Thanks to these data, they were able to establish a demand curve, and to determine consumer’s surplus (Hanemann, 2006; Pearce, 2002). Trice and Wood’s paper (1958), that mention Hoteling’s work, is known as the first published application of the TCM (Banzhaf, 2010; Hanemann, 2006). [SOURCE: Page_30]

Ciriacy-Wantrup (1947), proposed an alternative solution, based on interviews: addressing the issue of soil conservation measures, he realized that these measures induced nonmarket benefits and wanted to derive a demand curve for it: he suggested to ask people their willigness to pay for these benefits (Hanemann, 2006; Pearce 2002). This work (with the one of Bowen (1943)) built the foundations of the Contingent Valuation Method (CVM). The first application was undertaken by Davis (1963). The TCM and the CVM became the first members of the two big families of non-market valuation methods: the Revealed Preference Methods (RPM) and the Stated Preference Methods (SPM). The 1960 and 1970 decades were prosperous times for environmental economists (Méral, 2021) for several reasons: first, the rise of the environmental movement, fighting against ecosystem loss, came with the need of a better understanding of environmental issues and values (White, 1969). This was fuelled by various events and publications, like Rachel Carson’s classic book, Silent Spring^2 (Carson, 1962) and the groundbreaking club of Rome report The limits to growth (Meadows et al. 1972). [SOURCE: Page_30]

At the same time, Krutilla (1967) and Weisbrod (1964) contributed to the environmental valuation development by defining nonuse and option values. This generated a debate between economists to know if these values should be included in cost-benefit analyses: Rosenthal and Nelson (1992) argued that existence value should not: one of their worries, among others, was the difficulty to capture it accurately. Kopp (1992) directly addressed these criticisms. Weikard (2005) followed Rosenthal and Nelson (1992) views, and went even [SOURCE: Page_30]

2 Excerpts of the book were published in the New Yorker magazine, that contributed to its diffusion. See Tortell (2020). [SOURCE: Page_30]

28 [SOURCE: Page_30]

further, writing that bequest value should not be used to evaluate public projects. He stated that the distinction between use and nonuse values (NUV) is somewhat unclear, and that a bequest value is intrinsically related to some use: « If a good has no actual or potential use for some individual now or in the future, then it does not have a bequest value. Ultimately bequest value must be based on a use value for someone ». This point raises the question of the empirical definition and measurement of NUV. The difficulty is that users of an environmental good can also have a NUV (Marre, 2015). Strategies to target nonusers and NUV are discussed in Marre (2015), Johnston et al. (2005) and Cummings and Harrison (1995). The issue is important in environmental economics, as such values may be substantial (Marre, 2015; Kling et al. 2012). One of the most striking example results from the Exxon Valdez oil spill in 1989 (Kling, 2012): Hausman et al. (1995) assessed recreational use value damages due to the black tide and find an estimated value of $3,8 million, while Carson et al. (2003), focused on passive use value : the estimation was, this time, much higher: between $4,9 billion and $7,19 billion. In the middle of the 1980’s NUV started to be included in the what is called the total economic value (TEV) framework (Méral, 2021; Pearce 2002), that became the classic representation of environmental values in economics. [SOURCE: Page_31]

At the end of the 1980’ some authors started to emancipate themselves from the traditional environmental and ressources economics that draws from the neoclassical approach to give birth to ecological economics (EE) (Méral, 2021; Froger et al. 2012; van den Bergh, 2001; Costanza, 1989). According to the EE, the economic system at the heart of the neo-classical analysis is just a subsystem of a more global one, the biosphere. The objective in EE is to find metrics and indicators that take in consideration the functionning of that biosphere, in interaction with socio-economic dynamics (Méral, 2021; O’Hara, 2001). As a result, valuation exercises using only market values are considered to be too restrictive (O’Hara, 2001; Odum and Odum, 2000). Berta et al. (2021) sum up ecological economists views about values and valuation: [SOURCE: Page_31]

« […] ecological economics has been built on the idea of value pluralism, positing several intrinsic and incommensurable environmental values that cannot be reduced to a common economic one. Environmental economics, on the contrary, quickly assigned price or monetary valuation the decisive role it already played in resources allocation within neoclassical and welfare economics ». [SOURCE: Page_31]

Berta et al. (2021), p.18 [SOURCE: Page_31]

29 [SOURCE: Page_31]

This citation is quite explicit about ecological economists’ worries. They reject this tendency to reduce value to its monetary expression, i.e value monism, that can only capture one of the numerous relevant dimensions of nature’s value (Söderbaum, 2000; Georgescu-Roegen, 1982). The plural nature of environmental values leads to other critisisms from ecological economists as these values don’t necessarily share a common unit of measurement. Nature is seen as icommensurable (Funtowicz et al. 2002; Martinez-Alier et al. 1998) and environmental values may be only weakly comparable (Martinez-Alier et al. 1998) and even in conflict with each other (Funtowicz et al. 2002; Martinez-Alier et al. 1998). Pascual et al. (2017) and Gómez-Baggethun and Martín-López (2015) share examples of values that can be considered in valuation processes. [SOURCE: Page_32]

This tendency to use money as a valuation language was not restricted to economists: natural scientists started to adopt the monetary metric, as it was easily understandable for policy makers (Gómez-Baggethun and Martín-López, 2015). In ecological economics, if relations between researchers from natural sciences, such as Costanza, Ehrlich or Jansson and economists existed from the begining (Méral, 2021; Røpke 2004), some ecological economists wanted to preserve their specificities with respect to standard economic analysis. [SOURCE: Page_32]

Spash (2020, 2013) clarified the different existing positions: [SOURCE: Page_32]

First, scholars from natural sciences mainly defended a « New Environmental Pragmatism »: the objective of such an approach is to influence policy makers in order to protect biodiversity and ecosystems. This is the line with authors like Costanza and Daily; [SOURCE: Page_32]

The second one « New Resource Economics », follows neoclassical economic standards, accepting market prices as valid indicators of value. In line with Perrings (2006; 1997), they use mathematical formalism to « understand, model and predict the dynamics of coupled ecological–economic systems » (Perrings, 2006); [SOURCE: Page_32]

Finally, « Social Ecological Economics » is the one that stands out the most openly from economic orthodoxy, linking economics with a range of other disciplines such as philosophy, geography, politics and the natural sciences. As a result, value pluralism is accepted and multi- criteria analysis is necessary. This approach is promoted by Spash (2013). [SOURCE: Page_32]

If Spash classification is a subject of debates (see Lundgren, 2022 and Levrel and Martinet, 2021), the importance taken by the monetary valuation of ecosystem services after Costanza et al. (1997) publication is greatly acknowledged (Méral, 2021; Plumecocq, 2014; de Groot [SOURCE: Page_32]

30 [SOURCE: Page_32]

et al. 2012), with « new environmental pragmatists » having important responsabilities in that trend (Gómez-Baggethun and Martín-López (2015)). Nevertheless, the opposition against monetary valuation (see Norgaard and Bode, 1998) or even against valuation as a whole remained important: the most famous example is probably Heal (2000), following Vatn and Bromley (1994), who stated that: « Valuation is neither necessary nor sufficient for conservation. We conserve much that we do not value, and do not conserve much that we value ». To be fair, the use of ecosystem services valuation in decision-making is rather limited (Marre et al. 2016; Laurans et al. 2013). On the other hand (monetary) valuation is defended by authors such as Costanza et al. (1998), Daly (1998) and Herendeen (1998). One topic of controversy between supporters and opponents of monetary valuation concerns the issue of nature’s commodification i.e. the « expansion of market trade to previously non- marketedareas » (Gomez-Baggethun and Ruiz-Pérez, 2011) that could ultimately lead to the privatization or financialization of the environment (Smessaert et al. 2020). This sparked a debate on several aspects, including ethical concerns (Martin-Ortega et al. 2019; Gomez Baggethun and Ruiz-Pérez, 2011). Some authors see monetary valuation as a degree of commodification (Hahn et al. 2015) or as a process that can lead to it (Gomez Baggethun and Ruiz Pérez, 2011). However it does not mean that the two terms are equivalent (Costanza et al. 2014), as the first is necessary but not sufficient to acheive the second (Gomez-Baggethun and Ruiz Pérez, 2011). [SOURCE: Page_33]

### 5. Economic valuation of preferences: non-market valuation methods

Environmental and ecological economists tried to adress some of these criticisms, by developping an alternative framework, based on deliberative processes. Deliberative Monetary Valuation (DMV) can be seen as a range of approaches polarized between two archetypes: Deliberated Preferences (DP) and Deliberative Democratic Monetary Valuation (DDMV) (Kenter, 2020; Kenter et al. 2016). Kenter et al. (2016), describes the former as an adaptation of stated preferences methods to include deliberation processes to enhance individual preferences, while the latter elicit group values. Deliberative exercices based on stated preferences, such as discrete choice experiment, (DCE) can be found in Lliso et al. (2020) and Kenter (2016). [SOURCE: Page_33]

DCE fall into the SP methods category, that encompasses many different methods used in various disciplines (Holmes et al. 2017; Carson and Louviere, 2011). Ben Akiva et al. (2019) retraced the history of SP elicitation starting with Leon Thurstone’s paper (1931). Interest in [SOURCE: Page_33]

31 [SOURCE: Page_33]

SP methods originated with the theory of conjoint measurement pioneered by Luce and Tukey (1964) and Luce and Suppes (1965), and was further developed as conjoint analysis by market researchers like Louviere (1988). From a methodological point of view, It led to a semantic debate. Carson and Louviere (2011) tried to define a common nomenclature for SP methods: First, the term « conjoint analysis » (CA) was introduced to the marketing literature by Green and Rao (1971), and refers to the specific method of eliciting preferences derived from conjoint measurement (CM). The latter designates a « formal mathematical theory of ordered relations on sets of attribute levels that allowed one to identify and test various algebraic functional forms of decision rules » (Carson and Louvière, 2011). Therefore CM is not a behavioral theory of choice and do not rely on error theory, unlike random utility theory (RUT), that underpine DCE (Louvière et al. 2010). Carson and Louviere (2011) proposed the name « discrete choice experiment » to distinguish this method from other elicitation techniques, sometimes used in other disciplines. RUT was first developped by Thurstone (1927) and applied to econometrics by McFadden (1974), who developped the conditional logit model (CLM). CLM exhibits independence from irrelevant alternatives (IIA) (Ben- Akiva et al. (2019); Holmes et al. (2017)). This property states that the ratio of choice probabilities between two alternatives in a choice set is unaffected by other alternatives in the choice set. While this hypothesis might be relevant in some cases, it is not always the case, as pointed out by Chipman (1960) and Debreu (1960). Besides, with the CLM, parameters are assumed to be identical across respondents, i.e. respondents have homogeneous preferences (Hernandez and da Costa, 2022; Amiri et al. 2022). [SOURCE: Page_34]

To overcome these limitations, other models were developped: unlike the CLM, the mixed logit model (MLM) is based on the assumption that parameters are randomly distributed in the population. Then, preferences heterogeneity can be captured by estimating the mean and variance of the random parameter distributions (Holmes et al. 2017). While the CLM lack of correlation between alternatives consistent with the IIA property, the MLM allow for correlations among the stochastic part of utility. As a result MLM does not exhibit IIA (Holmes et al. 2017; Train, 2009). Train (2009), provides a detailled presentation of the MLM. [SOURCE: Page_34]

The MLM is not the only model to move away from the IAA assumption. The latent class model (LCM) offers an other alternative: in the LCM, individuals are sorted into a set of classes: the class assignment is unknown from the researcher (Greene and Hensher, 2003). One issue with the LCM is the number of classes: one common solution to choose is to use information criterias such as the Bayesian information criterion (BIC) and consistent Akaike [SOURCE: Page_34]

32 [SOURCE: Page_34]

information criterion (CAIC) (Pacifico and Woo, 2013). Weller et al. (2020) remind us that there is no consensus about the best criteria to be used. Besides, interpratibility of the results has to be taken into account as well. [SOURCE: Page_35]

DCE is now widely used in environmental valuation. However, they (and stated preference methods in general) have historically faced some criticisms^3: one of the main criticisms is associated to the possible hypothetical biais resulting from DCE : this biais can be defined as the difference between stated, hypothetical behaviour and actual behaviour in real markets (Haghani et al. 2021; Wuepper et al. 2019). Hopefully, DCE practicioners have developped tools to mitigate it, like cheap talk or consequentiality scripts (Wuepper et al. 2019). [SOURCE: Page_35]

Alternatively, reaserchers who want to avoid SP methods can rely on RP ones: this time preferences are not investigated through surveys. Preference elicitation comes from observing an actual behavior that is linked in some way to an environmental good: for example, the hedonic price method (HPM) often consider the housing market (Taylor, 2017). The choice of housing may depend on neighborhood amenities, in addition to house characteristics (the landscape for example: see Legras, 2016). Implicit prices for amenities of interest are therefore revealed through observable market transactions. Of course, as a consequence, one of the limits of this method is that, because it relies on housing transaction data, the HPM can only capture use values. On the contrary SP methods can be helpful to assess both use and nonuse values. [SOURCE: Page_35]

In the literature, some studies have compared SP and RP (Matos et al. 2013; Adamowicz et al. 1994), and, in particular DCE and HPM (Phaneuf et al. 2013). In this thesis, we follow this path, using both DCE and HPM to investigate environmental preferences. More specifically, the objective of this manuscript is to use various nonmarket valuation methods to value the co-benefits of the PSE scheme implemented on the Vittel watershed. [SOURCE: Page_35]

### 6. Organization of the manuscript

The manuscript is organized as follow: the first chapter aims at investigating the preferences of the local population around Vittel in term of agri-environmental measures: using a DCE, we asked respondents to choose between different recreational sites. The objective of this first [SOURCE: Page_35]

3 Others limitations include the cognitive burden associated with an heavy cognitive process when responding the DCE. [SOURCE: Page_35]

33 [SOURCE: Page_35]

chapter is to specifically investigate use values of the local population for environmental characteristics, that are similar to the one implemented in the Vittel PSE. [SOURCE: Page_36]

The second chapter uses the same method as the first, a DCE, but with a specific focus on nonuse values i.e the value individuals place on an environmental good even if they don’t actually use or come in contact with it, or expect to do it in the future (Segerson, 2017; Crowards, 1997). The first two chapters allow us to estimate the two components of the TEV: use values and nonuse values. [SOURCE: Page_36]

The third chapter is an application of the HPM on the Vosges departement. We used data from the Perval database that identify housing transactions from 1998 to 2021. To investigate what land use characteristics could impact house prices, we used the corine land cover (CLC) database that gives us the variation of land uses in the department for different periods (1990, 2000, 2006, 2012, 2018). We also look at the impact of different land uses on house prices in the Vittel watershed, to compare the trend with the rest of the department. This paper provides an alternative to SP methods. [SOURCE: Page_36]

The fourth and last chapter concerns data production describing the different databases constructed during the thesis project and giving dome descriptive statistics. These databases represent one of the main outputs of the PhD and this paper is a way to enhance this data. [SOURCE: Page_36]

34 [SOURCE: Page_36]

### 1. Introduction

Nestlé waters is the world leader in the sector of bottled water. In France, eight brands of mineral natural or spring waters are distributed by Nestlé Waters (Perrier®/Perrier Fines Bulles®, Vittel®, Hépar®, Contrex®, S. Pellegrino®, Nestlé Pure Life® and Acqua Panna®). Among these brands, Vittel® is probably one the most emblematic; and the brand name has always been “strongly associated with images of health and vitality” (Perrot-Maître 2006). The water springs are located on a watershed, called the Vittel impluvium, 4 which covers more than 10 000 ha. (Hellec, 2015; Frogneux and Benoît, 2019). More than the half of the land is used for the agricultural activity (Hellec, 2013; Frogneux and Benoît, 2019) and the forest surface represents 22% of the watershed (Frogneux and Benoît, 2019). [SOURCE: Page_53]

In 1988, the production unit of Vittel noticed a “deterioration in the quality of its mineral water, a slow but notably significant increase in nitrates”. (Déprés et al., 2005). The main cause was identified as “nonpoint source pollution from intensive farming practised in the fields surrounding the Vittel springs” (Déprés et al., 2005). One of the reasons of that pollution was identified as the production of corn, which is considered as an important factor in nitrate increase in groundwater (Deffontaines and Brossier, 1997; Perrot-Maître and Davis, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_53]

To address this problem, several alternatives were available to Nestlé Waters (see Déprés et al. 2005 for the details). One of these alternatives consisted in achieving contractual arrangement with farmers. After a collaboration between the French National Institute of Agronomic Research (INRA), Agrivair (a subsidiary of Nestlé Waters dedicated to these ecological issues), and local farmers, some concrete measures were adopted to guarantee the water quality: cessation of corn culture, composting of animal waste, etc. By doing so, the Vittel case became the “first recognized initiative” of Payment for Environmental Services (PES) in France (Hernandez and Benoit, 2011). The originality is that the PES scheme is supported by a private actor, Nestlé Waters-Agrivair and corresponds to a situation where direct payments by service beneficiaries are made to service providers, in which both providers and beneficiaries are private entities (Greiber, 2011). OECD (2005) and Perrot- Maître (2010) share the same conclusion: “in the presence of market failure, private transactions and voluntary approaches are more efficient, effective, equitable and sustainable than government approaches and need to be encouraged” (Perrot-Maître, 2010). If [SOURCE: Page_53]

4 Since ancient Roman times, the impluvium is the area in which rainwater is infiltrated. [SOURCE: Page_53]

51 [SOURCE: Page_53]

traditionally, market failures justify public intervention, Coase (1960) stated that these private arrangements might lead to an efficient solution when dealing with environmental issues, if transaction costs are sufficiently low (see Déprés et al. (2008) for a discussion about the transaction costs’ issue in the Vittel case). Accordingly, “Coasean” PES (Diswandi, 2017; Wunder et al. 2020; Bingham, 2021) correspond to “those that emerge from contractual transactions between private actors (i.e., providers and users of a service) in the absence of overt government intervention” (Bingham, 2021). Thompson (2021), detailed characteristics of such schemes, also called “user-financed” or “privately-funded” PES (Bingham 2021; Ezzine-de-Blas, 2016) and Ezzine-de-Blas (2016) gave some examples. If the Vittel arrangement is often cited as a real-world application of the Coase theorem (Déprés et al. 2005, 2008; Engel, 2016; Karsenty, 2017, Deryugina, 2021). Bingham (2021), argue that it cannot be called “perfectly Coasean” because the bargaining process constitutes a substantial departure from the theory. Besides, he conceded that user-financed agri-environmental watershed PES, as the Vittel Case, remain rare, and review cases that share similar aspects, but has also distinctive characteristics. Among them, The Volvic watershed management program offers an interesting comparison point (and contrast) with Vittel, still involving a private company, (Danone) but adopting a different approach (Chevrier et al. 2017; Trémolet et al. 2019). More generally, if private sector experiences in land reclamation and ecosystem recovery are more and more frequent (see Mulongoy and Fry, 2016), The scarcity of empirical case studies of Coasean PES is at odds with the importance of the theorem in the neoclassical and institutional economics, which the PES relies on (Bingham, 2021; Hecken and Bastiaensen, 2010). [SOURCE: Page_54]

According to the United Nations Economic Commission for Europe (UNECE, 2007) “Economic analysis is an essential tool for efficient decision-making regarding the establishment of PES schemes. It provides a coherent framework that allows a comparison of the costs and benefits of changes in water-related ecosystem services in an integrated manner”. These benefits are larger than water quality preservation (Hérivaux and Grémont, 2015, 2019; Hérivaux and Maréchal, 2019) and the attention about co-benefits that results from PES schemes is rising (Leonardi et al. 2018). One of the reasons for that is that they enforce the acceptability and effectiveness of the overall scheme (Leonardi et al. 2018). These benefits can be observed at a local or a global level, and in some cases, “a local activity has national or global implications” (Lipper et al. 2009). Identifying these benefits is therefore an important issue. [SOURCE: Page_54]

52 [SOURCE: Page_54]

For example, in the case of Vittel, according to Perrot-Maître (2010), “the impairment of the water quality would have eventually led to the closing down the natural mineral water business in the region that would also have affected the economy”, including local farmers, employment and more global economic activity. The development of the local biodiversity is another good example of a positive externality: “[…] successful PES schemes re-enforce the multifunctional role of ecosystems (through co-benefits) and highlight the economic and social benefit, which increase the acceptability and effectiveness of the overall scheme”. In the case of Vittel, “measures specifically targeting biodiversity protection were also supported by Nestlé, insofar as these were functional to the realisation of the core objective of sustainable farming practices to maintain high-quality mineral drinking water” (Dupuis and Vinuales, 2013). The economic literature that aims at valuing marginal benefits of ES that results from particular management actions or policy interventions (Christie and Rayment, 2012; Bateman et al. 2013), such as watershed management practices (Garcia- Llorente et al. 2012; Clarke et al. 2015; Castro et al. 2016a, 2016b), is growing. In the context of optimal watershed management, Dixon (1987, 2000) discussed the role of economists in a multidisciplinary approach, arguing that the monetary value of environmental benefits (and costs) of these management practices should be included within the analysis (Valatin et al. 2022). Accordingly, valuation papers often try to assign a monetary value to ES related to watershed management (Garcia-Llorente et al. 2012; Clarke et al. 2015; Castro et al. 2016b). In the literature, the concept of ES has numerous definitions. A major difference between these definitions lies in the existing relation between “services” and “benefits” (Nahlik et al. 2012). Some authors assume that the two notions are equivalent (Costanza et al. 1997; MEA, 2003; Harrington et al. 2010), while, for others (Boyd and Banzhaf, 2007; Fisher et al. 2009) benefits and services are distinct, the latter representing the point where human well-being “is directly affected” (Fisher et al. 2009), often by combination of ecological processes of an ecosystem and of other forms of capital or resources (Boyd and Banzhaf, 2007; Fisher et al. 2009; Lamarque et al. 2011; INRA, 2017). Following the first approach, recreation is a cultural ES, i.e., a “nonmaterial benefit obtained from ecosystems” (MEA, 2003). Considering the second framework, recreation is only a benefit, jointly produced (Fisher et al. 2009) with water quality preservation, through more environmental friendly practices. Because outdoor recreation partially rely on the characteristics of the natural environment (alongside with others forms of capital), considering recreational activities is also a mean to estimate the value people give to some of its components, such as biodiversity or landscapes. [SOURCE: Page_55]

53 [SOURCE: Page_55]

In order to give a value to the environmental and social benefits jointly provided with the preservation of water quality, we propose to use a discrete choice experiment (DCE). This method, “generally considered as […] appropriate […] for the valuation of multi-attribute nonmarket goods” (Tu et al., 2016), is based on the idea that any environmental good can be described in terms of attributes, and in terms of levels these attributes can take (Hanley et al., 2001; Birol and Koundouri, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_56]

We test various scenarios of recreational activities considering diversity of landscapes (i.e., countryside and forest) and thus different recreational sites. We made that distinction between forest and the countryside for at least two reasons. First, forest and agricultural lands cover a majority of the impluvium. Second, both are common settings for recreation. While most of attributes are common to both scenarios, some are also specific to the forest and others to the countryside. [SOURCE: Page_56]

The other originality of our study is that, besides “classical” attributes characterising the recreation location choice such as recreation facilities (e.g., picnic tables, bins, information signs) or marked hiking and biking trails, we specifically test attributes related to the different measures set up by Agrivair, such as absence of pesticide use, less intensive livestock production, less cereal cultivation with the introduction of crop rotation,5 hedgerows planting in fields, water course maintenance. The absence of pesticides for protecting water springs are perceived by recreational users as a cleaner air. The reduction of livestock and cereal fields imply land use changes and thus landscape changes while hedgerows and mixed tree species in forests are specifically described as habitats for biodiversity. This design of DCE makes it possible to estimate the willingness to pay (WTP) for these different environmental and social attributes in order to elicit their different use values and to study the relationships between users’ recreational preferences and the water company environmental objectives. [SOURCE: Page_56]

Furthermore, for some attributes, e.g., pesticides, people are not aware about their presence or not. Hence, we test whether this information issue has an impact on their choice of recreational site. [SOURCE: Page_56]

This paper is organised as follows: in the next section, we present our methodology by describing the literature related to recreation activities and DCE, the experimental design, and [SOURCE: Page_56]

5 From the beginning of contractualisation between Agrivair and farmers, the suppression of maize cultivation was part of the good agricultural practices. [SOURCE: Page_56]

54 [SOURCE: Page_56]

the survey carried out in region of Vittel in France. In section 3, we present the empirical application. In section 4, we detail the estimation results. Section 5 concludes the paper. [SOURCE: Page_57]

### 2.1.        Recreation activities and DCE

Nonmarket valuation, as based on individual choices and preferences underlying those choices, is one (but not the only) way that researchers and practitioners have sought to define and measure the values that individuals assign to environmental goods and services. Standard economic theory defines value in terms of the trade-offs that individuals are willing to make between different situations. The value of a good or a service, estimated as the variation of its quantity (or the improvement or a degradation of its quality in a case of environment and natural resources), is the maximum amount of another good that an individual would be willing to give up in exchange of an improvement of that is being valued. [SOURCE: Page_57]

We apply a stated preference approach, i.e., DCE where the respondents reveal their preferences by choosing between different alternatives. According to Holmes et al. (2017), “there has been an explosion of interest” in DCE during the past two decades. The method began to be applied in the early 80’s (Da Costa and Hernandez, 2019) with the works of Louviere and Hensher (1983, 1989), and Louviere and Woodworth (1983). DCE progressively became more popular than any other environmental valuation methods (Guijarro and Tsinaslanidis, 2020) including contingent valuation (Mahieu et al. 2014; Guijarro and Tsinaslanidis, 2020). The paper that “generated attention in the environmental economics community” (Carson and Czajkowski, 2014) was the one of Adamowicz et al. (1994), who used both a DCE and revealed preference data about recreational trip destinations to value environmental amenities. [SOURCE: Page_57]

Since then, the DCE technique had been used in ES valuation, including recreation (Boxall and Macnab, 2000; Christie et al. 2007; Hasan-Basri et al. 2015). Extensive farming systems can favour recreation and generate many positive externalities and has oven been evaluated by stated preference methods (Novikova, 2014). DCE have been suitable to investigate preferences for agricultural landscapes characteristics (Grammatikopoulou et al. 2012; Häfner et al. 2018; Schaak and Musshoff, 2020). DCE has also shown to be useful to understand farmers’ decisions, such as the choice to adopt environmentally friendly practices (Chèze et al. 2018) or agricultural production technologies (Jaeck and Lifran 2012). [SOURCE: Page_57]

55 [SOURCE: Page_57]

In this paper, we implement a DCE to estimate the use values associated with different attributes of recreational activities for the local population around the Vittel impluvium. We use choices of recreational sites to collect information about respondents’ preferences for recreational activities according to some relevant attributes already identified in previous studies (e.g., facilities such as picnic tables or information signs, hiking paths, see e.g., Abildtrup et al. 2013) and attributes based on specific measures implemented by Agrivair (e.g., no use of pesticides, hedgerows planting). We consider two types of recreational sites: the countryside and the forest. Respondents are asked to choose between two hypothetical sites, one referring to the countryside and the other one referring to the forest, and a status- quo alternative, which is the last visited forest or countryside. Before the experimental part of the survey, the respondents had to indicate if their last visit was in a forest or in the countryside, and to describe the site based on the attributes considered in the DCE. Previous studies suggest that the decision of using labelled or unlabelled alternatives is important (Jin et al. 2017; Hensher et al. 2015; Doherty et al. 2011; Blamey et al. 2000). Both labelled and unlabelled DCE present advantages and disadvantages (Chakir et al. 2016; Blamey et al. 2000). In a recreation site choice context, one of the main advantage of using labels is that it helps reflecting better the “emotional context” (Blamey et al. 2000) in which the decision choice occurs, making the choice situation more realistic (Kruijshaar et al. 2009; de Bekker- Grob et al. 2010). For example, in recreation studies, an individual can have a predisposition to favour a site because of (good) memories from a past visit (Blamey et al. 2000). Besides, the label itself conveys information (de Bekker-Grob et al. 2010; Blamey et al. 2000), and these labels may be used by respondents as a way to infer information that they perceive as missing (de Bekker-Grob et al. 2010). On the other hand, individuals sometimes largely, or even only, rely on the name of the alternative when making a choice (Doherty et al. 2011, Müller et al. 2020). Nevertheless, Doherty et al. (2011) argue that it does not disqualify labelled DCE as they are likely to be “the correct mechanism to model realistic choices”. The choice between labelled and unlabelled DCE ultimately depends on the research question, on the type of respondents (de Bekker-Grob et al. 2010) and on the objective of the study (Blamey et al. 2000). Because the first option present several advantages in recreational site choice studies (Blamey et al. 2000; Doherty et al. 2011), we chose to use three labelled alternatives: the “last forest or countryside visited” scenario (i.e., the status quo), the “(hypothetical) countryside” scenario, and the “(hypothetical) forest” scenario. [SOURCE: Page_58]

56 [SOURCE: Page_58]
