### 2.2.        Study area and experimental design

We may expect the local population to derive value from the Vittel impluvium as a recreational site for several reasons. First, forests (Hummel 1992, Lindhagen 1996, Roovers et al. 2002) and the countryside (Goossen and Langers 2000; De Vries et al. 2007) are generally popular places for recreation in Europe, and France is not an exception (Peyron et al. 2002). The impluvium is located in a rural area with a non-negligible forest surface,6 which is largely appreciated by local residents as a recreational site. Secondly, at the local level (in Lorraine), forest frequentation is historically more important than, on average, in the rest of France (Abildtrup et al. 2012). Concerning rural areas, the inhabitants can benefit from a preserved natural environment, with six natural parks in the Grand Est region, 8 which are appreciated by residents (Tardieu and Tuffery, 2019; INSEE, 2010) and share common characteristics with the Vittel impluvium in terms of land cover (Tardieu et Tuffery, 2019) and recreational facilities.7 [SOURCE: Page_59]

A literature review was the first step to select the relevant attributes. Because the Vittel case is known as the first example of PES in France (Hernandez and Benoit, 2011), the scientific literature on that case is quite important (see Perrot-Maître, 2006, Chia and Raulet Croset, 1994, among others). These papers help to identify the services jointly produced by water protection measures, like the preservation of biodiversity or landscape quality. Moreover, the existing literature on DCE for recreational activities gave us some inspiration to think about the levels to consider in our study (Carson et al. 1990, Boxall et al., 1996, Bateman et al., 2003 and Christie et al., 2007). [SOURCE: Page_59]

The following tables present the eight attributes selected in our DCE and the attribute levels. Some of the selected attributes are common to the forest and to the countryside (Table 1.1); some others are specific either to the countryside or to the forest (Table 1.2). [SOURCE: Page_59]

6 Nestlé Waters’ private forest is located near the city of Vittel, at the heart of the catchment basin that encompasses some 10,000 hectares, approximately 25% of which are covered with forest. 8 https://www.pnr- lorraine.com/comprendre/le-parc/les-parcs-naturels-de-la-region-grand-est/ 7 https://www.meusetourism.com/en/take-a-deep-breath/the-lorraine-regional-country-park.html [SOURCE: Page_59]

57 [SOURCE: Page_59]

Table 1-1 : Attributes common to the countryside and the forest [SOURCE: Page_60]

Table 1-2 : Attributes specific to the countryside and to the forest [SOURCE: Page_60]

58 [SOURCE: Page_60]

In order to see if the chosen attributes and levels were relevant and the questionnaire understandable for members of the local population, two focus groups were organized, on June 3, 2019. These sessions included a presentation of the survey, the completion of the questionnaire, a session with questions, comments and suggestions from the respondents and finally the participants had to write on a paper what was the attribute they appreciate the most during a recent recreational trip in the area. [SOURCE: Page_61]

One session took place during the morning, the other during the afternoon with six people in each group. Among the 12 respondents, all were local residents, six worked for Agrivair, three for Nestlé Waters, and the others were farmers or peasants. Based on comments and discussion with the participants, we were able to make some modifications (on form and substance) in order to make the questionnaire as clear and as relevant as possible. [SOURCE: Page_61]

Notably, we made some modifications of the attributes levels and how they were presented. For example, considering the fact that corn cultivation has a negative impact on water quality, we first chose to include a corn attribute in the design. However, during the focus-group period, some respondents argued that looking only at this particular culture was too restrictive. Consequently, we decided to include other forms of agricultural land use, like the presence of livestock or grasslands. [SOURCE: Page_61]

Regarding the number of attribute levels, half of the attributes (i.e., facilities, marked hiking and biking trails, water streams, and pesticides) are binary. These attributes correspond to the presence or the absence of the attribute on the recreational site. We chose to use more levels for three attributes, considering their complexity (i.e., hedgerows and biodiversity, agricultural land use, and tree species). The first two are specific to the countryside, the last one only concerns the forest: [SOURCE: Page_61]

Finally the distance attribute has eight modalities: 1, 5, 10, 15, 25, 35, 50 or 70 km. The information collected about the distance that people accept to travel will be used to estimate the monetary value they give to the attributes by converting travel distance to travel costs. We chose to use the round-trip distance that respondents travel to go to a recreational site as an indirect monetary attribute. This procedure has several advantages: first, not using a direct monetary attribute reinforces the realism of the choice situations, as individuals are not always used to pay to have access to a recreational site (Rulleau et al. 2010). Moreover, the distance is seen as an important attribute that can strongly influences people decision when choosing [SOURCE: Page_61]

59 [SOURCE: Page_61]

a recreational site (Agimass et al. 2018). Finally, a monetary value can still be derived from the distance attribute afterwards (Hanley et al. 2002). [SOURCE: Page_62]

Researchers use an experimental design “to map attributes and levels into sets of alternatives to which respondents indicate their choices” (Johnson et al. 2013). The design aims at isolating the effects of individual attributes on the choices (Hanley et al. 1998). ”Efficient experimental designs maximize the precision of estimated choice-model parameters for a given number of choice questions” (Johnson et al. 2013). In our case, we generated 24 choice sets, which were allocated to three blocks with eight choice sets to reduce the number of choices per respondent. An efficient statistical design was estimated applying NGENE (ChoiceMetrics 2014). We used the literature and focus groups to define expected effect (signs) of the different attributes. [SOURCE: Page_62]

Each choice set was composed of three alternatives: a status-quo option, a “forest” alternative and a “countryside” one. Including a status-quo alternative in DCE is generally recommended (Penn et al. 2019; Marsh et al. 2011; Hensher, 2010; Adamowicz and Boxall, 2001) for various reasons, such as making choice tasks more realistic or avoiding forced choices that can lead to biased welfare estimates. Different possibilities, presented by Hess and Rose (2009), are offered to researchers when they decide to propose such an option. One of them is to include one alternative (or more) describing respondents recent experience with the good of interest (Hess and Rose, 2009). Several studies illustrated this case where the alternative is respondents-specific (Banzhaf et al. 2002; Hensher and Greene, 2003; Hensher, 2004; Birol et al. 2009; Barton and Bergland, 2010; Tu et al. 2016; Glenk 2011), and more specifically for the assessment of recreation preferences (Banzhaf et al. 2002; Tu and Abildtrup, 2016). We used an experienced status quo that allows to reduce the hypothetical character of choice sets, by making it more familiar and realistic (Bradley 1988; Glenk, 2011; Haghani et al. 2021). In a valuation context, familiarity with the environmental good is known to be a good predictor of response reliability (Loomis and Ekstrand, 1998). Using respondents’ knowledge to construct alternatives raises the question of the level of information available to respondents about the good to be valued.8 [SOURCE: Page_62]

8 This issue has been addressed by the information economics literature: Nelson (1970, 1974) and Darby and Karni (1973) set up the “Search, Experience, Credence” (SEC) classification framework, based on the availability of information (about the products and services) to consumers. [SOURCE: Page_62]

60 [SOURCE: Page_62]

Information is an important issue in recreation site choices: information levels, sources (and therefore credibility) and timing but also the ability of an individual to use this information are key factors (Pigram and Jenkins, 2006) 9. However, because respondents do not always have all the information when they select a recreational site (see Adamowicz et al. 1997), stated preference survey instruments have to be designed to consider uncertainty. Methods available to do so had first been discussed in the contingent valuation field, as Arrow et al. (1993) recommended to include a “no-answer” option in such surveys (Haab and McConnell, 2002). Different possibilities are offered to researchers in order to deal with uncertainty : the inclusion of a “don’t know” or a “not sure” options (as an alternative to yes or no answers) or the use of a certainty scale in follow-up questions are common solutions (Samnaliev et al. 2006; Balcombe and Fraser 2009). Some authors decided to use both (Samnaliev et al. 2006; Whitehead and Cherry, 2007). However, Balcombe and Fraser (2009) indicated that “don’t know” options are quite rare in DCE. Exceptions can be found in MacDonald et al. 2005; Balcombe and Fraser 2009; Tu and Abildtrup 2016). In our DCE design, we chose to include it within our respondent-specific status quo, as a level by letting the respondent indicate if not knowing the attribute level of the specific site visited the last time. [SOURCE: Page_63]

In our study, the status quo is based on information about the last visit made by the respondents in a forest or in the countryside during the last 12 months (or during the last five years if no visits have been made last year) with a recreational goal. If the respondents did have no recreational trips during the last five years, they did not answer to the DCE. The two others alternatives are generated on a hypothetical basis, but with the same attributes. Table 1.3 shows an example of a choice set. Each attribute is illustrated with a pictogram, even in the status-quo alternative. The choice of using monochrome pictograms and not photos was motivated by the desire to not influence the answers. [SOURCE: Page_63]

9 The treatment of that information is made through a complex process called “cognition” that involves “acquisition, coding, storage, recall and manipulation of information” (Walmsley, 2003). Two sorts of knowledge derives from that cognition process: “figurative knowledge”, resulting from direct contact with a situation or a good (with a recreational site for example) and operative knowledge, coming from known situations to infer knowledge about situations yet to be experienced (Walmsley, 2003). More specifically, the link between the information available to an individual and his/her recreation behaviour has been addressed since the late 70’s (Aldskogius, 1977; Lime and Lucas, 1977; Schreyer et al. 1984). [SOURCE: Page_63]

### 2.3. Survey

This step concerns the practical implementation of the survey. The first issues are sampling and the geographical targeting. Considering the fact that the aim was to survey the local population around the two municipalities Vittel and Contrexéville, we established a perimeter of 15 kilometres, corresponding to the Vittel life area. In that perimeter, municipalities (except [SOURCE: Page_64]

62 [SOURCE: Page_64]

the largest ones, i.e., Vittel, Contrexéville and Mirecourt), were randomly selected 10, see Figure 1.1. The following step was to establish a rule to construct a 600 household’s sample: we chose to survey 5% of the households in the three largest municipalities (in red in Figure 1.1) and 10% in the others (in yellow) (see Table 1.4). Originally, 13 municipalities and 624 households were included in the sample. Because of the containment linked to the coronavirus pandemic, we were only able to survey 588 respondents. Figure 1.1 shows the municipalities that belong to the sample, and the sites that respondents visited for a recreational purpose during the last five years (inside and outside the impluvium). [SOURCE: Page_65]

Figure 1-1:Map of the municipalities included in the sample [SOURCE: Page_65]

10 The reason for focus the interviews in randomly selected municipalities with in the 15 km zone was to reduce the travelling of the interviewers between interviewed households. This was the reason for increasing the sampling rate outside the main towns (Contrexéville, Mirecourt, and Vittel). [SOURCE: Page_65]

63 [SOURCE: Page_65]

Note. Total number of municipalities = 13. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Table 1-4 : Sample and randomly selected municipalities [SOURCE: Page_66]

We worked with the company Wood’up to implement the survey. As we chose to do it in face to face, two surveyors were hired to visit the respondents with tablets. This process allows them to answer potential questions and to help respondents with the tablets if necessary. To make sure that the households were randomly selected, we constructed a random protocol. Following this idea of randomization, the person allowed to answer to the questionnaire was the last adult (available) in the household to have celebrated his or her birthday. In order to facilitate the implementation of the survey, a flyer of presentation of the survey had been distributed in the selected municipalities. Finally, feedbacks were organized on a regular basis with the surveyors to monitor the progress of the survey. [SOURCE: Page_66]

### 3.1.          The random utility model

DCE has a theoretical grounding in Lancaster’s characteristics theory of value (Lancaster, 1966), and an econometric basis on random utility models (RUM, Thurstone, 1927; Mcfadden, 1973; Manski, 1977): RUM aims at modelling the choices of individuals among discrete sets of alternatives 𝑗. These models assume that the preferences of an individual among the available alternatives can be described by a utility function. [SOURCE: Page_66]

64 [SOURCE: Page_66]

In a given sample with 𝑁 respondents, each respondent 𝑛 faces 𝑇 choice situations. Every choice situation has a choice set of 𝐽 alternatives. The total utility for respondent 𝑛 choosing alternative 𝑗 in the choice set in situation 𝑡 is 𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡. This utility depends on a component that the researcher can observe, called the deterministic part of utility attributes (𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡) and a random component that is not observed ( 𝑛𝑗): [SOURCE: Page_67]

𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡 = 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡          𝑛𝑗𝑡, 𝑛 = 1, … , 𝑁,           𝑗 = 1, … , 𝐽     𝑡 = 1, … , 𝑇                               (1) [SOURCE: Page_67]

The deterministic part 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 is a function of 𝑋𝑛𝑗𝑡 the vector of attributes relating to alternative 𝑗, and other observed factors such as individual characteristics 𝑍𝑛:11 [SOURCE: Page_67]

𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 = 𝑉(𝑋𝑛𝑗𝑡, 𝑍𝑛)                                                                                            (2) [SOURCE: Page_67]

At this stage, the functional specification 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 is very general. However, it is useful to give a linear form to the deterministic part and to separate the price attribute from the non-price attributes to compute preference space and WTP space, as done in Train and Weeks (2005). [SOURCE: Page_67]

The random error term 𝑛𝑗𝑡 is i.i.d. as a Gumbel law (or type I extreme value).12 The relative contribution of each attribute 𝑋𝑘𝑛𝑡 to the overall utility 𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡 can be represented by a parameter 𝛽𝑘𝑛, while the parameters 𝛾𝑙 are associated with the 𝑙 individual characteristics 𝑍𝑛. From here on, we consider that the utility parameters to be estimated are embedded in a vector of parameters 𝜃𝑛, associated with the regressors 𝑅𝑛𝑗𝑡 = (𝑋𝑛𝑗𝑡, 𝑍𝑛). [SOURCE: Page_67]

The individual 𝑛 chooses the alternative 𝑖 in each choice set 𝑡 that brings the highest utility: [SOURCE: Page_67]

𝑈𝑛𝑖𝑡 ≥ 𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡,   ∀𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝑡, 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗.                                                                          (3) [SOURCE: Page_67]

Unlike the utilities, only the choice preferred by the individual 𝑛 is observed. Hence, we intend to estimate the probability of individual 𝑛 choosing alternative 𝑖 for each choice set 𝑡: [SOURCE: Page_67]

𝑝𝑛𝑗𝑡 = Pr(𝑈𝑛𝑖𝑡 ≥ 𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡) = Pr(𝜀𝑛𝑗𝑡 − 𝜀𝑛𝑖𝑡 ≤ 𝑉𝑛𝑖𝑡 − 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡),                                                   (4) [SOURCE: Page_67]

11 In our empirical application, we also add fixed effects specific to the municipalities where the household is living to account for unobserved heterogeneity related to municipalities. 12 The Gumbel density of the error term of utility is exp (− 𝑛𝑗𝑡) exp[ −exp (− 𝑛𝑗𝑡)] and the cumulative distribution [SOURCE: Page_67]

isexp [−exp (− 𝑛𝑗𝑡)], with a variance of 𝜋2⁄6. [SOURCE: Page_67]

65 [SOURCE: Page_67]

where the new error term 𝜖𝑛𝑖𝑡 = 𝑛𝑗𝑡 − 𝑛𝑖𝑡 is i.i.d. with a logistic cumulative distribution [SOURCE: Page_68]

### 3.2.           Econometric specifications

To explain the choices of the respondents and to interpret the results different econometric models can be used according to Train (2003), the multinomial logit model, also called the conditional (multinomial) logit (CL) is the most frequently used model to explain discrete choices. The probability of household 𝑛 choosing alternative 𝑖 can be written as: [SOURCE: Page_68]

exp(𝜃𝑅𝑛𝑖) 𝑃𝑛(𝑖) =     𝐽                                                                                (5) ∑𝑗=1 exp(𝜃𝑅𝑛𝑗) [SOURCE: Page_68]

This model relies on the assumption of independence of irrelevant alternatives (IIA). This assumption states that the odds of the probability of any two alternatives chosen by the respondent are independent of the presence of any other alternatives in the choice set (Hensher et al. 2005). Moreover, the CL model assumes that the utility functions across respondents are identical, which means that preferences must be homogeneous. Other models, such as the mixed logit (MXL) models, try to address these issues by allowing parameters to vary randomly over individuals. It is generally assumed that preferences vary across respondents but not across choices of the same respondent. Hence, “a clustered specification is applied that allows for repeated choices for each individual” (Revelt and Train, 1998). In our study, we estimate both CL and MXL models to test the robustness of our results. [SOURCE: Page_68]

The probability of an individual n to choose alternative 𝑖 conditional on knowing 𝜃𝑛 can be expressed by: [SOURCE: Page_68]

(6) [SOURCE: Page_68]

In the case of multiple choices for each respondent, the logit probability refers to the probability that the individual n makes a sequence of T choices specified as 𝑡 = {1, … , 𝑇}. [SOURCE: Page_68]

66 [SOURCE: Page_68]

Knowing the probability of each choice as presented by equation (6), the logit probability of the observed sequence of 𝑇 choices is given by: [SOURCE: Page_69]

(7) [SOURCE: Page_69]

where 𝑗𝑛𝑡 represents the alternative chosen by individual n in choice situation t. The unconditional logit probability that individual n makes the observed sequence of choice j is integrated over the distribution of 𝜃: [SOURCE: Page_69]

(8) 𝐿𝑛(𝜃) = ∫ 𝑃𝑛(𝑗𝑛1, … , 𝑗𝑛𝑇𝑛|𝜃𝑛)𝑓(𝜃𝑛|𝜇, Σ)𝑑𝜃 [SOURCE: Page_69]

In a MXL model, the mean 𝜇 and the variance–covariance matrix Σ of the distribution of utility parameters 𝜃𝑛 can be specified with a continuous distribution, such as normal, lognormal or triangular. The log-likelihood is maximized using maximum simulated likelihood methods (Train, 2003). In our paper, we used the statistical software STATA MP 16 and the mixlogit package (Hole, 2007). [SOURCE: Page_69]

Note that the CL model can be written from equation (5) by replacing 𝜃𝑛 by 𝜃, where preferences are considered as homogenous and 𝜃 is the same for all individuals. We use the clogit command of STATA, which manage fixed effects and thus well adapted to our dataset where individuals makes a sequence of T choices. [SOURCE: Page_69]

### 3.3.         Descriptive statistics

The results presented here are based on 588 fully completed questionnaires. First, we present some statistics about respondents’ preferences in terms of type of recreational site (countryside or forest), favourite characteristics, etc. [SOURCE: Page_69]

During the last five years period, 208 out of 588 respondents (i.e., 35% of the sample) did not visit neither the countryside nor the forest. Table 5 shows for the recreational activities of the 379 respondents who did at least one visit either over the last 12 months or during the last five years.13 Among these, 94.7% actually did at least one visit during the last 12 months. A little bit less than the half of them (45%) visited the forest and the countryside several times. 39% [SOURCE: Page_69]

67 [SOURCE: Page_69]

only visited the forest and 16% only visited the countryside. Among people who made at least one visit per year, the average number of visits (nine respondents did not answer the question), either in a forest or in the countryside over the last year is 30.42. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Figure 1.2 summarizes choices made by the respondents in the DCE. Out of these 3032 choices (i.e., 379 individuals responding to eight choice sets), the (hypothetical) alternative “countryside” had been chosen 869 times (Figure 3). It represents about 28.7% of the choices versus 26.5% for the hypothetical forest. That means that the status quo is clearly the favourite option (about 44.8% of the choices). This prevalence of the status quo has been termed “status- quo effect” or “status quo bias” by Samuelsson and Zeckhauser (1988). This dominance of the status quo is not systematic (Hanley et al. 2006) but not uncommon neither in DCE studies (Hartman et al. 1990, 1991; Boxall et al. 2009, Lanz and Provins, 2015), and has even found to be stronger in other papers (Hartman et al. 1990, 1991; MacDonald et al. (2005); Barton and Bergland, 2010; Lanz and Provins, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_70]

68 [SOURCE: Page_70]

Sample: 673                   Fully completed :                  Usable for DCE questionnaires                        588                          analysis: 379 [SOURCE: Page_71]

Status quo: 44.8% 3032 observations                                     Hypotetical forest: 26.5% Hypotetical countryside: 28.7% [SOURCE: Page_71]

Figure 1-2: Sample, number of observations and choices [SOURCE: Page_71]

Furthermore, 35 out of the 379 respondents (9.21%) did not consider all the attributes when choosing. Distance^14 and facilities were the most ignored characteristics. In addition to the question relative to attribute non-attendance (ANA), other validation or “follow up” questions (Byrd et al. 2017; Kataria et al. 2017; Melo Guerrero et al. 2020) were included, in order, for example, to investigate status-quo bias: we found that 11,08% of respondents systematically chose that status-quo option. This result has to be compared with the one of other studies: using a choice modelling approach to assess customers’ services standards in urban water, MacDonald et al. (2005) found that the share of respondents that systematically chose the status-quo option was slightly inferior to 20% (with 6 choice tasks). In environmental valuation, Meyerhoff and Liebe (2009) concluded that respectively 59% and 49% of their two samples always chose that option. They compared these results about changes in forest biodiversity with those of Lehtonen et al. (2003) who wrote that “only” 14% of respondents always chose the status quo. Even closer to our subject, Horne et al. (2005) examined visitors’ preferences for forest management regimes, and found that 21% of surveyed sample always selected the current situation. Compared to these studies, the share of systematic status quo in our study appears quite reasonable. When they justified this systematic behaviour, more than the half of respondents (57%) argue that the status quo always presented the best characteristics according to them. [SOURCE: Page_71]

Then, we focused on the travel distances (table 6), as these data will be crucial to give a monetary value to the environmental and social services (using a travel cost methodology). We chose not to consider a few respondents who travelled on very important distances [SOURCE: Page_71]

### 4.     Estimation results

Based on the responses to the DCE, we estimated the RUM. The estimated parameters indicate how the attributes (characteristics) of a recreational site influence the utility of visiting the site. In other words, if the estimated parameter is positive it is an attractive characteristic for an average visitor and if it is negative, the attribute has a negative impact on the utility/preferences of the visitor. [SOURCE: Page_73]

We estimated simple econometric specifications in which the indirect utility is a linear function of parameters. From the general deterministic part 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 defined in equation (2), we started with a basic specification with the attributes as explanatory variables, as well as an alternative specific constant (𝐴𝑆𝐶𝑆𝑄), associated with the status-quo alternative (𝑆𝑄). This variable equal to 1 when the last visited site is preferred to other alternatives, and to zero 71 [SOURCE: Page_73]

otherwise. We also introduced a variable referred to as 𝐹𝑂𝑅𝐸𝑆𝑇 that indicates when the respondent chose either the “forest scenario” or the status-quo alternative when she/he visited a forest during the last recreational visit. We called 𝐴𝑆𝐶𝐹 its associated parameter. In the following specification of the utility part 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡, we separate the distance attribute (𝐷𝐼𝑆𝑇) and the other attributes 𝑋 to make the analysis of WTT (Willingness to Travel) clearer:16 [SOURCE: Page_74]

𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 = 𝐴𝑆𝐶𝑆𝑄 × 𝑆𝑄 + 𝐴𝑆𝐶𝐹 × 𝐹𝑂𝑅𝐸𝑆𝑇 + 𝛽𝑋𝑛𝑗𝑡 − 𝛼𝐷𝐼𝑆𝑇                                                         (9) [SOURCE: Page_74]

In a second specification, we augmented the model with respondent characteristics 𝑍𝑛 as interactions with the 𝑆𝑄 dummy as well as interaction terms between 𝐷𝐼𝑆𝑇 and the fixed municipality-specific effects (𝑀𝑈𝑁𝐼𝐶𝐼𝑃) in order to account for unobserved local heterogeneity (such as other recreational opportunities): [SOURCE: Page_74]

𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 = 𝐴𝑆𝐶𝑆𝑄 × 𝑆𝑄 + 𝐴𝑆𝐶𝐹 × 𝐹𝑂𝑅𝐸𝑆𝑇 + 𝛽𝑋𝑛𝑗𝑡 − 𝛿𝐷𝐼𝑆𝑇 × 𝑀𝑈𝑁𝐼𝐶𝐼𝑃 + 𝛾𝑍𝑛 × 𝑆𝑄                                    (10) [SOURCE: Page_74]

We do not introduce all municipality variables in the regression because the number of individual observations per municipality is sometimes small and the number fixed effects would be high. Instead, we preferred test the effect of the larger municipality in our sample (i.e., Vittel, Contrexéville, Mirecourt) and to gather the other smaller municipalities in one category. [SOURCE: Page_74]

### 4.1.       Estimation results of the CL model

Table 1.9 presents estimation results of the CL model, with the basic specification (9) in the first two columns, and the specification (10) in the last two columns. For the estimation, we removed the 12 individuals who stated distances strictly greater than 200km (for the round trip) because we consider that these trips are exceptional recreation activities that people do not repeat several weekends during the year.17 Higher pseudo R squared and smaller AIC and BIC confirm that a model augmented with the crossed terms fits the data better than a model considering only attributes of the DCE. [SOURCE: Page_74]

16 We do not have a monetary attribute in our experiment but instead we asked the respondent to state the distance to travel towards the recreation site, making it possible to compute a WTT. 17 In our study, we investigate the attractivity of the impluvium for the local population. We decide not to consider recreational sites at more than 100km as local substitutes for residents. [SOURCE: Page_74]

The results show that the coefficient associated with the attribute Facilities is not significantly different from zero. Therefore, we cannot conclude that the presence of facilities on a recreational site contribute to visitors’ welfare. Even if some past studies suggest that respondents often value recreational facilities (De Valck et al. 2017; Hasan-Basri 2015), others are more nuanced, pointing out that individuals can associate the provision of facilities with a risk of artificialization (Rulleau et al. 2010). However, not knowing about the presence of facilities decreases the utility of interviewed people. As expected, trails and water runs are appreciated for recreation. On the contrary, the value of the coefficient associated with Pesticides is negative, as well as the value for PesticidesDK, which means that people who answered to the DCE prefer not to have recreation activities on sites where they do not know if pesticides are used. If we have a closer look to the characteristics that are specific to the countryside (agricultural land use, hedgerows and biodiversity), we see that none of the different levels have a significant impact on recreational preferences. However, concerning the forest attribute, the results show a preference for forest exclusively composed of deciduous trees compared to those composed of coniferous. [SOURCE: Page_76]

To investigate how location may influence the effect of distance on respondents’ utility, we created interaction terms between the variable DIST, that represents the round-trip distances to recreational sites, and the municipalities of the sample. All the coefficients of these interaction terms are negative, which means that, as expected, the distance has a negative impact on utility. However, the negative effect of distance on utility is significantly larger for Contrexéville and Vittel than for the other municipalities. Finally, we also generated interactions between 𝐴𝑆𝐶𝑆𝑄 and the socio-economic variables, as a way to account for observed or “systematic” heterogeneity (Hasan-Basri, 2015). Most of the interaction terms are significantly different from zero. Among others, having grown up on the territory where you live has a positive impact on choosing the (same) last visited site. Older people also keep on the same site as well as people with a higher degree of study. There are also significant differences of preference according to the socio-professional category of interviewed individuals. [SOURCE: Page_76]

### 4.2.         Estimation results of the MXL model

In the estimation of the MXL model, we assumed normal distributed parameters for all attributes except the distance and interaction terms for which we assumed non-random parameters. [SOURCE: Page_76]

We can notice that the MXL model fits the data better than the CL models (in Table 1.9) for a number of reasons. First, most of estimates (mean and standard deviation) of the random parameters associated with the attributes are significantly different from zero, thus confirming the presence of individual heterogeneity in preferences. Second, comparing loglikelihood and statistics such as AIC and BIC between the different models shows the superiority of MXL on CL models with the highest log-likelihood and the lowest AIC and BIC. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Besides, the robustness of the results is quite strong, considering both the sign and the value of the coefficients. Nevertheless, a few changes can be observed: the coefficient of livestock relative to grassland is now significantly different from 0 and negative, meaning that the individual utility is decreasing with livestock. The coefficient associated with a low level of hedgerows is significantly positive, indicating a preference for a low level of hedgerows (and biodiversity) relative to no hedgerow. However, interviewed people do not seem to value a high level of hedgerows even if heterogeneity of preferences for this attribute is large as indicated by its significant standard deviation. Rambonilaza and Dachary-Bernard (2007), pointed out that density of linear elements, like hedgerows, was important to make a substantial contribution to landscape attractiveness. In the other hand, van Zanten et al. (2016) argue that landscape preferences are place specific and can vary across region. As a confirmation, when deriving WTP estimates for landscape improvements under Agri Environmental Schemes (AES), Campbell et al. (2006), found that among eight landscapes attributes, hedgerows was the least valued (Birol and Koundouri, 2008; Campbell et al. 2006). Moreover, the coefficients of Deciduous forest and Mixed forest are now both significantly positive relative to Coniferous forest. [SOURCE: Page_78]

Finally, the values of the interaction terms between the distance attribute and the municipalities are still negative and highly significant, which confirms the robustness of that result. This attribute allows us to calculate marginal willingness to travel (MWTT) for each attributes of recreational sites. [SOURCE: Page_78]

76 [SOURCE: Page_78]

### 4.3.           Estimation of marginal willingness to travel

As we explained above, we chose to use the round-trip distance between the residence and the recreational site as an indirect monetary attribute. Therefore, we can estimate the marginal willingness to travel, or MWTT (EFESE, 2020). The MWTT is calculated as a ratio of the coefficient 𝛽𝑘 associated with the non-monetary attribute 𝑋𝑘 and the coefficient 𝛼 associated with the variable 𝐷𝐼𝑆𝑇. Thus, the MWTT can be written as follows: 𝛽 𝑀𝑊𝑇𝑇 = −                                                                                           (11) 𝛼 [SOURCE: Page_79]

We calculated this ratio for the same attributes presented in the MXL model. The values are given for the three largest municipalities (Vittel, Mirecourt and Contrexéville) and for the small municipalities. MWTT as well as their estimated confidence intervals are reported in Table 1.11, and the confidence intervals are estimated with the delta method. [SOURCE: Page_79]

To convert these values in terms of WTP, we can simply use a cost per kilometer travelled: 0,55€/km, as given by the French 2020 mileage scale. 18 Then marginal WTP (MWTP) for each attribute is given by the following formula: [SOURCE: Page_80]

𝑀𝑊𝑇𝑃 = 𝐷𝑖𝑠𝑡𝑎𝑛𝑐𝑒 × 0.55€ /𝑘𝑚                                                                         (12) [SOURCE: Page_80]

MWTT for facilities are not significantly different from 0. MWTT for trails are positive but close from 0, and with large confidence intervals. Respondents show a real preference for the presence of water runs, but the value differ depending on the municipality of residence. Indeed, while inhabitants of Vittel and Contrexéville have relatively weak MWTT, those of Mirecourt and other municipalities express stronger MWTT, 7.65 km (approximatively 4.20€) and 12.32 km (or 6.78€). We found the same distinction for the “pesticides” attribute: Residents of Vittel and Contrexéville have smaller MWTT to go to a recreational site where pesticides are not used, 8.89 km (4.89€) and 6.17 km (3.39€) respectively, when this value is much more important for individuals who live in Mirecourt (32.59 km or 17.92€) or in the other rural municipalities (52.46 km or 28.85€). We can notice that people who live in these small rural municipalities also have an important MWTT to a site where they know that pesticides are not used (relatively to a site where they don’t know if this kind of products is used), i.e., 28.69 km (15.78€) but with a large confidence interval. In this respect, the heterogeneity for all these attributes is important. [SOURCE: Page_80]

Considering agricultural land use, the MWTT for mixed land use is higher for Mirecourt and the small municipalities than for Vittel and Contrexéville: Inhabitants of Mirecourt are ready to pay almost 10€ (or 17.79 km) and residents of the other rural municipalities, almost 16€ (28.64 km). The values for a low level of hedgerows are even more important, respectively 19.46 km (10.70€) for Mirecourt and 31.32 km (17.23€) for smaller rural municipalities, respectively. Lastly, MWTT for a forest attributes are also more important for the same [SOURCE: Page_80]

18 We have taken a value per km of 0.55€ according to the French 2020 mileage scale for the mileage allowance at real costs for a car with a fiscal power of 5CV on the following website: https://impots.dispofi.fr/baremekilometrique/bareme-2020. [SOURCE: Page_80]

78 [SOURCE: Page_80]

municipalities, 21.23 km (11.68€) and 34.17 km (17.79€) for a forest with exclusively deciduous trees, and 14.31 km (7.87€) and 23.03 km (12.67€) for a mixed forest. [SOURCE: Page_81]

To enforce the validity of our results, we also asked respondents to design their “ideal” forest or countryside, depending on where their last visit took place. After the DCE, we proposed them to rank the five most important attributes for them, following the approach used by Balcombe and Fraser (2014), who advocated for the use of that exercise in DCE. One small difference is that these authors strictly kept the same attributes than in the DCE, while we add other characteristics: it allowed us to see if some attributes not included in the DCE were important to respondents. Alternatively, Filyushkina et al. (2017) presented the same type of “ideal forest” exercise in a recreational context, but based on drawings. [SOURCE: Page_81]

We divided our sample in two parts: if respondents’ last visit took place in the forest, they had the opportunity to select their five favourite forest attributes. If they went to the countryside, they did the same for this recreational setting. Concerning the forest, the hiking and biking trails was the attribute more often ranked first. In terms of number of appearances in the top five, the trails are also the most appreciated one. Concerning the countryside, the facilities were more often ranked first and appeared in the top five more frequently as well. These results may be quite surprising, regarding the estimations of the CL and MXL models. Both in the CL and in the MXL model, the coefficient associated to facilities is not significantly different from zero. This result may reflect the fact that facilities are indeed more appreciated in the countryside than in the forest. As a confirmation, the attribute “facilities” is only the fifth (out of eleven) in terms of number of appearances in the top five for the forest. This could explain non-significance, but it does not mean that facilities are not valued. As Balcombe and Fraser (2014) put it, ranking questions allow respondents to “place a lower value on particular attributes without assuming that they have zero value”. The same reasoning can be applied for the favourite attribute in the forest (hiking and biking trails). We can therefore conclude that our results display important heterogeneity, depending on the recreational setting. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Besides, several studies (see Caparrós et al. (2008) for a review) indicated that results obtained from a ranking and from a choice exercise may not be similar. This can be due to a different cognitive process between the two tasks (Boyle et al. (2001); Holmes and Boyle (2001)). [SOURCE: Page_81]

79 [SOURCE: Page_81]

### 5. Conclusion

This paper aimed at providing results of a DCE used to evaluate the environmental and social preferences of the local population of Vittel (France) and surroundings. We detailed the construction process of the DCE based on the recreational activities practiced by the residents (sport, landscape observation etc.) when they go to the forest or in the countryside. Focusing on these activities is a way to capture the direct use values potentially modified by the environmental friendly practices implemented on the impluvium of Vittel (non-use of pesticides, hedgerows plantation etc.). The contribution of our study to the literature is twofold: first, we specifically include in the DCE attributes linked to the different measures set up by a private actor, Agrivair, such as absence of pesticides, or hedgerows plantation. Furthermore, because people are not aware about the presence or not of some characteristics on the site they visit, e.g., pesticides, we test whether this information issue has an impact on their choice of recreational setting. [SOURCE: Page_82]

The results show that the status quo (i.e., the last visited forest or countryside) is the favourite scenario. Among the hypothetical ones, the “countryside scenario” is, by a small margin, the most chosen. Concerning the number of visits during the last 12 months, almost the half of the respondents who visited nature for recreational purpose did several visits in the forest and the countryside. The mean number of visits, regardless the type of recreational area, is about 30 visits/person/year. The estimation results show that the presence of some attributes, like water runs, on recreational sites, are particularly appreciated by respondents. In other cases, like for mixed agricultural land use, preferences are quite heterogeneous. Besides, the calculation of WTP for the different attributes indicates some (important in some cases) variations between Vittel and Contrexéville, on the one hand, and smaller municipalities in the other hand. Both results seem to exhibit heterogeneity among respondents in terms of recreational preferences. [SOURCE: Page_82]

80 [SOURCE: Page_82]

Q40) Nous réalisons le questionnaire auquel vous répondez actuellement afin de mieux connaître les préférences de la population locale concernant les activités récréatives (loisirs). Dans ce but, nous avons distingué, deux types d'espaces récréatifs : la forêt et la campagne. Nous allons vous demander de choisir, selon vos préférences, entre différents scénarios : ces scénarios seront composés de différentes caractéristiques, et reflètent ce que vous pouvez trouver (ou non) en forêt et où à la campagne. Les caractéristiques pourront prendre différents niveaux, qui seront illustrés par un pictogramme. Certaines caractéristiques seront communes à la forêt et à la campagne (présence de sentiers de randonnée balisés par exemple) et d'autres seront spécifiques à la première ou à la seconde (la caractéristique "occupation des parcelles agricoles" sera, par exemple, spécifique à la campagne). Voici les caractéristiques communes aux 2 types d'espaces (les attributs spécifiques à la campagne et à la forêt seront décrits dans les pages suivantes). [SOURCE: Page_96]

94 [SOURCE: Page_96]

non utilisation de pesticides [SOURCE: Page_97]

Q50) Ceci est un exemple de choix que vous aurez à effectuer lors des 8 questions suivantes. Vous devez effectuer ce choix selon vos préférences, en fonction des caractéristiques proposées. Pour cela, vous devrez sélectionner, en bas de la page, le scénario choisi. Le scénario "dernière forêt ou campagne visitée" présente les caractéristiques du dernier site que vous avez visité. Les icônes qui apparaîtront dans cette colonne sont donc celles que vous aurez sélectionné au cours des questions précédentes. Vous devrez comparer ces caractéristiques à celles qui vous sont proposées dans les scénarios "campagne" et "forêt" pour faire votre choix. Pour les caractéristiques qui n'apparaissent pas ici (densité de la forêt, par exemple, le cas échéant), imaginez qu'elles sont également similaires à celles de la dernière forêt ou campagne visitée. De la même manière, veuillez considérer que le contexte (vacances, week-end etc.) et la finalité (promenade, cueillette etc.) de votre sortie sont les mêmes. Ce qui est présenté ici est juste un exemple. Quand vous avez compris la démarche à suivre, passez directement à la question suivante, sans choisir de scénario. La question est présentée comme ceci : "veuillez sélectionner un des scénarios comme si vous aviez eu le choix, avant votre dernière visite, entre ces trois options". [SOURCE: Page_103]

### 1. Introduction

In this paper, we estimate preferences for different measures to protect water resources and biodiversity, focusing on the preferences of non-users. The usefulness of ecosystem service (ES) valuation for measures to protect and restore aquatic environments has recently been recognised in France (Amigues and Chevassus-au-Louis [2011]). While water management policies initially focused mainly on water quality and availability, the good ecological status of water bodies has gradually become an important issue, in particular with the publication of the European Water Framework Directive (WFD) in 2000. Although the WFD does not explicitly mention the notion of ES (Amigues and Chevassus-au-Louis [2011]), actions taken to achieve good water status have a direct impact on ecosystems (Salvetti, [2013]; CGDD, 2014). However, the WFD can be seen as the starting point for the use of economic analysis in water management in France (MEDD [2005]), in particular through cost-benefit analysis (CBA) (MEDD [2005]; Salvetti [2013]; CGDD [2014]; Feuillette et al. [2015, 2016]). Such analysis includes environmental benefits (and costs) for which non-use values (NUVs) can be significant (MEDD [2005]). [SOURCE: Page_133]

Over the years, the measurement of NUVs has become an important issue and environmental economists have developed valuation methods to account for these values (Adamowicz et al. [1998]; Pearce [2002]; Banzhaf [2016]). Historically, the contingent valuation method (CVM) has been widely used to value NUVs (Bateman and Langford [1997]; Adamowicz et al. [1998]; Colarullo and Perez [2003]; Kafy et al. [2018]), although the method has been questioned (Desvouges et al. [1993]; Desvouges et al. [2010]). Comparing the ability of CVM and discrete choice experiments (DCE) to assess such values, Adamowicz et al. [1998] concluded that DCE performed well. In addition, Alpizar et al. [2001] suggest that DCE is less subject to the warm glow effect than CV when making such valuations. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Following Adamowicz et al. [1998], some authors estimated only NUVs: for example, Windle and Rolfe [2005] assessed only NUVs for the protection of the Fitzroy estuary in central Queensland, Australia. Others, such as Johnston et al. [2005] and Moore et al. [2018], chose to examine both. An advantage of this approach is that it allows comparisons to be made between the two groups (users and non-users). Whether researchers want to estimate use values only or both use values and NUVs, they need to decide how to define non-use and non- users in order to distinguish them from use (and therefore users). [SOURCE: Page_133]

131 [SOURCE: Page_133]

Traditionally, the literature has identified two approaches to determining NUVs (Marre et al. [2015]; Johnston et al. [2003]). The first is to target a population that will certainly never use (e.g. because of distance) and then ask them about their willingness to pay (WTP). The second is to have both users and non-users in the sample and estimate values for both groups. Both approaches raise definitional issues (Marre et al. [2015]; Johnston et al. [2005]). In general, the literature defines non-users as individuals who have not used a resource in a defined historical period, and do not expect to use it in a similar way in a defined future period (Johnston et al. [2003]). Users, on the other hand, are typically defined in terms of their participation in (recreational) activities at the site of interest during a defined period of time: There is no consensus on the time period to be considered: Moore et al. [2018] conducted a DCE in the Chesapeake Bay estuary in the United States, estimating the WTP for improvements in five environmental attributes for a sample of users and non-users. The former were defined as individuals who participated in recreational activities at the Bay within the last five years. Amiri et al. [2022] considered the same time lag in their DCE study specific to recreational use values, while Farber and Griner [2000] considered the last two years. Finally, Tu et al. [2016] focused on the last 12 months. Alternatively, in a DCE on salt marsh restoration efforts in Rhode Island, Johnston et al. [2005] distinguished between recreational nonusers (who did not visit the site for recreational purposes) and complete non-users (who did not use the site for recreational purposes and also did not drive by (regularly) or live within sight of the Rhode Island salt marsh). Respondents who did not fall into either of these categories were considered users. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Numerous applications of stated preference methods have been used to assess non-market values of watersheds or river basins (Loomis [1997]; Duberstein and de Steiguer [2003] and Thurston et al. [2009]). If contingent valuation has been widely used in the past (Duberstein and de Steiguer [2003]) and is still being used (Abebe et al. [2019]), the DCE method has become popular for conducting such valuations. Case studies of DCE assessed preferences for watershed/river basin ES can be found all over the world (Kaval [2019]): in Europe (Garcia Llorente et al. [2012]; da Costa et al. [2019], Amiri et al. [2022]), in the United States (Shrestha and Alavalapati [2004]; Chaikaew et al. [2017]; Aguilar et al. [2018]) or in Asia (Cheng et al. [2021]; Khan et al. [2019; 2021]. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Most of this literature investigated local population preferences for ES or land use management (Amiri et al. [2022]; Cheng et al. [2021]; da Costa et al. [2019] Chaikaew et al. 2017; Garcia Llorente et al. [2012]). However, some authors used a different strategy: Aguilar [SOURCE: Page_134]

132 [SOURCE: Page_134]

et al. [2018] conducted a national survey on ES from forested watersheds and attitudes towards payment for environmental services (PES) programmes. Moore et al. [2018] conducted a DCE on water quality improvements in the Chesapeake Bay, surveying residents of the 17 eastern states and the District of Columbia to enable NUV assessment: about 70% of respondents were non-users of the Chesapeake Bay or watershed lakes. However, the authors found no distance decay in WTP. [SOURCE: Page_135]

The existence of distance decay in NUVs, where the value decreases with distance to the environmental good being valued, has been widely discussed in the literature (Glenk et al. [2020]; Johnston et al. [2015]) and the empirical results are mixed (Johnston et al. [2015], Schaafsma et al. [2013]). Farber and Griner [2000] tested the existence of a distance effect for both users and non-users in a study of watershed quality improvements for two streams in western Pennsylvania. They found that non-users placed low and statistically insignificant values on stream improvements, and that these values were unrelated to distance from the streams. They interpreted this as consistent with a 'purist' interpretation of NUVs, which suggests that NUVs must be spatially independent to be clearly distinguished from use values. Hanley et al. [2003] tested whether the distance decay effect is different for a single environmental good (the Mimram River) than for a larger set of goods (all rivers in the Thames region). Specifically, they estimate Tobit regressions where the dependent variables are positive WTP as a function of individual characteristics and distance to the environmental good, depending on use values and NUVs. [SOURCE: Page_135]

Our study aims at assessing preferences and in particular NUVs for environmental improvement projects in a specific watershed using a DCE. We want to know if these values exist and what their amplitude is for each of the following water and biodiversity protection measures: reduction of use of pesticides and fertilisers, protection and planting of hedgerows, restoration of watercourses and sustainable forest management. Another focus is the existence of a distance effect on environmental preferences, by interviewing people who live either near or far from the watershed. Assessed WTP is useful for public decision-makers to know which protection measures to target, and could help to guide and calibrate public taxes or subsidies. [SOURCE: Page_135]

133 [SOURCE: Page_135]

### 2.1.        DCE and watershed management

Our study assesses preferences for NUVs of watershed water and biodiversity protection measures by targeting non-users. We use a stated preference approach, i.e. DCE, in which respondents reveal their preferences by choosing between different alternatives. According to Holmes et al. [2017], « there has been an explosion of interest » in DCE during the past two decades. The method began to be applied in the early 80s (Da Costa and Hernandez, [2019]) with the works of Louviere and Hensher [1983] and Louviere and Woodworth [1983]. DCE gradually became more popular than all other environmental valuation methods (Guijarro and Tsinaslanidis [2020]), including contingent valuation (Mahieu et al. [2014]; Guijarro and Tsinaslanidis [2020]). The paper that “generated attention in the environmental economics community” (Carson and Czajkowski [2014]) was the one of Adamowicz et al. [1998], who used both a DCE and revealed preference data on recreational destinations to value environmental amenities. [SOURCE: Page_136]

As written previously, DCE investigated preferences for watershed/river basin ES in a variety of context and countries (Kaval [2019]): in Europe (Garcia Llorente et al. [2012]; da Costa et al. [2019], Amiri et al. [2022]), in The United States (Shrestha and Alavalapati [2004]; Chaikaew et al. [2017]; Aguilar et al. [2018]) or in Asia (Cheng et al. [2021]; Khan et al. [2021, 2019]). Even if these watersheds/river basins might share some characteristics, every site is unique and different factors, such as land use/ land cover features, may influence ES provision (Postel et Thompson [2005]; Smith et al. [2006]; FAO [2006]). [SOURCE: Page_136]


---

### 2.2.        Study area and experimental design

We designed the DCE to estimate the economic value of protecting water quality and biodiversity in a particular watershed, focusing on the NUVs. However, groundwater protection has direct use values for water consumers, and biodiversity protection may increase recreational value for local landscape users. Therefore, to estimate the NUVs, we identified a specific watershed in a rural area with low population density and clearly defined water users. To make our scenarios as realistic as possible, we specified hypothetical measures for the Rupt de Mad watershed located in the Rhin-Meuse Basin (Figure 2.1), which is currently part of a project to improve biodiversity and water quality. Residents consuming drinking water [SOURCE: Page_136]

134 [SOURCE: Page_136]

from this watershed were then excluded from the sample (residents of Metz and surrounding municipalities). [SOURCE: Page_137]

a) [SOURCE: Page_137]

b) [SOURCE: Page_137]

Figure 2-1: a) Outline of the Rhine-Meuse Basin (BRM) in blue and b) the Rupt de Mad watershed in red [SOURCE: Page_137]

We surveyed a representative sample of residents in the area covered by the Rhin-Meuse water agency. Water agencies (Agences de l'eau) are French public institutions created in 1964 to manage and preserve water resources and the aquatic environment. Inspired by foreign initiatives (Brun [2012]; Barraqué et Laigneau [2017]), the French water policy remains somewhat unique (Feuillette et al. [2015]): in fact, the 1964 law is also the origin of other governance structures, such as the basin committee (Brun [2012]; Feuillette et al. [2015, 2016]). The French territory is divided into six river basins, and each one has a consultative structure (Comités de bassin) and an executive one (Agence de l'eau). According to the polluter-pays principle, the agencies receive royalties from water users (compagnies, households, etc.) depending on the level of water pollution and the extraction of resources (Brun [2012]; Feuillette et al. [2015]). These fees are then used to finance projects to improve water management and quality at the level of the river basin. This reciprocity is referred to as the "water-pay-water" principle (Brun [2012]; Barraqué and Laigneau [2017]). Such projects are selected according to criteria defined in a multiannual programme and validated by a basin committee (Brun [2012]; Feuillette et al. [2015]). The water agency therefore has a credible framework for introducing hypothetical protection scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_137]

135 [SOURCE: Page_137]

In our questionnaire, we first presented the context of the watershed improvement projects to the respondents, i.e. the role of the Rhin-Meuse water agency in the protection of water resources and biodiversity. We explained that they participate in the financing of projects to promote practices that are more favourable to water quality (less intensive use of pesticides, etc.) and the protection of biodiversity. The respondents are informed that such measures contribute to improve the quality of the raw water, thus reducing the cost of water treatment, which benefits consumers in Metz and the surrounding communes. As these residents are excluded from the analyses, our sample only includes respondents who do not directly benefit from improved water quality. [SOURCE: Page_138]

The attributes included in the DCE were selected through a literature review and discussion with stakeholders and agricultural scientists (Table 1.1). These included five attributes related to agricultural and forest land uses, the dominant land uses in the catchment: pesticide use, watercourse restoration, type of fertilizer used on agricultural land, hedgerows and forest management. The sixth attribute, the mean of payment, was an increase in the annual water bill (corresponding to the current practice of financing environmental measures) between 1 and 55 €. The levels of the different attributes refer to environmental measures that the project plans to implement or not: for example, watercourse restoration (narrowing of streams to allow faster water flow, planting of trees, etc.) may or may not be included in the project. The number of levels considered depends on the attribute: attributes related to pesticides, renaturation and forest management have only two levels each, while hedgerows management and fertilizer application have three levels. Including an additional level for these attributes allowed us to consider a variety of possible management practices. [SOURCE: Page_138]

Table 2-1 : Description of the attributes of the DCE [SOURCE: Page_139]

137 [SOURCE: Page_139]

Researchers use an experimental design “to map attributes and levels onto sets of alternatives from which respondents indicate their choices” (Johnson et al. [2013]). The design aims to isolate the effects of individual attributes on choices (Hanley et al. [1998]). In our case, we generated 24 choice sets, which were divided into three blocks of eight choice sets to reduce the number of choices per respondent. An efficient statistical design was estimated using NGENE (ChoiceMetrics [2014]). The design was estimated using priors based on a previous study in the same region (Amiri et al. [2022]) and discussions with stakeholders. [SOURCE: Page_140]

Table 2.2 shows an example of a choice card: respondents are given three alternatives. The status quo is based on the absence of an environmental project. Including a status quo alternative in the DCE is generally recommended (Penn et al. [2019]; Marsh et al. [2011]; Hensher [2010]) for various reasons, such as making choice tasks more realistic or avoiding forced choices that can lead to biased welfare estimates. [SOURCE: Page_140]

### 3. Empirical Analysis

DCE has its theoretical foundation in the new Lancaster's Theory of Value (Lancaster [1966]), and its econometric basis is the Random Utility Model (RUM, Thurstone [1927]; Mcfadden [1973]; Manski [1977]): RUM aims at modelling the choices of individuals among discrete sets of alternatives 𝑗. It assumes that preferences for the different alternatives can be described by a utility function. In a given sample of 𝑁 respondents, each respondent 𝑛 faces 𝑇 choice situations. Each choice situation has a choice set of 𝐽 alternatives. The total utility for respondent 𝑛 choosing alternative 𝑗 in the choice set in situation 𝑡 is 𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡. This utility depends on a component that the researcher can observe, called the deterministic part of the utility attributes (𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡) and a random component that cannot be observed (𝜀𝑛𝑗): [SOURCE: Page_141]

139 [SOURCE: Page_141]

𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡 = 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 + 𝜀𝑛𝑗𝑡, 𝑛 = 1, … , 𝑁,                      𝑗 = 1, … , 𝐽 𝑡 = 1, … , 𝑇                             (1) [SOURCE: Page_142]

The deterministic part              is a function of         , the vector of attributes relating to alternative , and other observed factors such as individual characteristics              : [SOURCE: Page_142]

𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡 = 𝑉(𝑋𝑛𝑗𝑡, 𝑍𝑛)                                                                                           (2) [SOURCE: Page_142]

The random error term                is i.i.d. as a Gumbel law (or type I extreme value).19 The relative contribution of each attribute                to the total utility       can be represented by a parameter , while the parameters         are associated with the individual characteristics                . From here on, we consider that the utility parameters to be estimated are embedded in a vector of parameters         , associated with the regressors                           . [SOURCE: Page_142]

The individual chooses the alternative in each alternative in each choice set that provides the highest utility: [SOURCE: Page_142]

𝑈𝑛𝑖𝑡 ≥ 𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡, ∀𝑖, 𝑗 ∈ 𝑡, 𝑖 ≠ 𝑗.                                                                                 (3) . [SOURCE: Page_142]

In contrast to utilities, only the choice preferred by individual                 is observed. Therefore, we want to estimate the probability that individual                 chooses alternative for each set of choices : [SOURCE: Page_142]

𝑝𝑛𝑖𝑡 = Pr(𝑈𝑛𝑖𝑡 ≥ 𝑈𝑛𝑗𝑡) = Pr(𝜀𝑛𝑗𝑡 − 𝜀𝑛𝑖𝑡 ≤ 𝑉𝑛𝑖𝑡 − 𝑉𝑛𝑗𝑡),                                                        (4) [SOURCE: Page_142]

where the new error term                                  is i.i.d. with a logistic cumulative distribution: [SOURCE: Page_142]

commonly used model to explain discrete choices, can be described by the probability that an individual       chooses alternative in choice situation : : [SOURCE: Page_143]

However, the CL model assumes independence of irrelevant alternatives (IIA) and that respondents' utility functions are identical, implying that preferences must be homogeneous, so that the parameter associated with the regressors are constant: [SOURCE: Page_143]

Other models, such as mixed logit (MXL) models, attempt to address these issues by allowing parameters to vary randomly across individuals (𝜃𝑛). It is generally assumed that preferences vary between respondents, but not between choices made by the same respondent. Therefore, « a clustered specification is applied that allows for repeated choices for each individual » (Revelt and Train [1998]). [SOURCE: Page_143]

The conditional probability of the MXL model can me written as following: [SOURCE: Page_143]

exp(𝑅𝑛𝑖𝑡𝜃𝑛) 𝑃𝑛𝑡(𝑖|𝜃𝑛) =𝐽                                                                             (6) ∑𝑗=1 exp(𝑅𝑛𝑗𝑡𝜃𝑛) [SOURCE: Page_143]

In the case of multiple choices for each respondent, the logit probability refers to the probability that the individual n makes a sequence of T choices specified as 𝑡 = {1,… , 𝑇}. Knowing the probability of each choice as presented by equation (6), the logit probability of the observed sequence of 𝑇 choices conditioned on 𝜃𝑛 is given by: 𝑇𝑛 [SOURCE: Page_143]

𝑃𝑛 (𝑗𝑛1 , … , 𝑗𝑛𝑇𝑛 |𝜃𝑛 ) = ∏ 𝑃𝑛𝑡 (𝑗𝑛𝑡 |𝜃𝑛 )                                              (7) 𝑡=1 [SOURCE: Page_143]

In our study, although we use the CL and MXL models to test the robustness of our results, our DCE data are analysed using a Latent Class Model (LCM) (see for example Greene and Hensher [2003]). Unlike the MXL model, which assumes continuous heterogeneity, the LCM is based on a discrete distribution, where heterogeneity is captured by class membership. The latter model is preferred to identify different groups of respondents whose preference variations are attributed to individual characteristics. [SOURCE: Page_143]

141 [SOURCE: Page_143]

First, a fractional multinomial logit is used to determine the probability for an individual 𝑛 to be in class 𝑐, based on her/his individual characteristics 𝑍𝑛: [SOURCE: Page_144]

exp(𝑍𝑛 𝜃𝑐 ) 𝜋𝑛𝑐 = ∑𝐶                     , 𝑐 = 1, … , 𝐶                                                     (8) 𝑐=1 exp(𝑍𝑛 𝜃𝑐 ) [SOURCE: Page_144]

For each class 𝑞, the probability of choosing alternative 𝑖 is estimated using a CL model: [SOURCE: Page_144]

exp(𝑅𝑛𝑖 𝜃𝑐 ) 𝑃𝑛 (𝑖|𝑐) =    𝐽                                                                                 (9) ∑𝑗=1 exp(𝑅𝑛𝑗 𝜃𝑐 ) [SOURCE: Page_144]

In our paper we used the statistical software STATA MP 18 and the lclogit^2 command (Yoo [2020]). We also used the mixlogit package (Hole [2007]) and the clogit command. 3.2 Descriptive statistics [SOURCE: Page_144]

The descriptive statistics on individual characteristics presented in Table 2.3 are based on the 977 questionnaires that were suitable for analysis. Figure 2.2 shows the step of managing the responses to the questionnaires and summarizes the choices made by the respondents in the DCE. Out of 1093 questionnaires, 1006 were fully completed. We also removed people who didn't understand the DCE exercise, based on their self-reports on follow-up questions to the DCE. [SOURCE: Page_144]

142 [SOURCE: Page_144]

Figure 2-2: Sample, observations and geographical distribution [SOURCE: Page_145]

Initially, the survey and the DCE were only intended for people living in the Rhine-Meuse Basin (BRM), where everyone pays the same charges directly on their water bill, which is collected by the water authority. However, people outside the BRM were also interviewed. This allows us to test whether the latter have understood the lack of credibility of the payment method, since they are not affected by the BRM agency taxes, but by those of their water agency. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Because we focused on NUVs, we removed from the sample individuals who were identified as water users.20 47 respondents, from 13 different municipalities were in this case. We also separated people who are not located in the BRM, because they are not likely to pay for environmental projects through a water bill increases. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Respondent giving the following reasons for always choosing the status quo were excluded from the sample: (i) Protest responses: “These projects should not be financed by increasing water bills”, “These costs must be borne by the landowner”, “I already pay too much for my water”, or (ii) misunderstanding : “The number of attributes to be compared was too large”, “I didn't understand what was being asked of me”. The analysis of non-attendance in attributes showed that a large majority of respondents took all characteristics into account when answering the DCE, regardless of whether they lived in the BRM or not. ### 4. Estimation results


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]

### 4.1.        CL and MXL models

Based on the responses to the DCE, we report the estimation results of the CL and MXL models in Table 2.5. First, we take advantage of having data from respondents outside the BRM to test whether they view the DCE differently, in particular knowing that the tax on the water bill is only introduced for people inside the BRM. To do this, we introduce the interaction term (water bill x BRM) in both logit models (in columns 1 and 3). A significantly negative parameter (added to the negative parameter of the water bill) indicates that respondents inside the BRM are more sensitive to the price and will therefore have a lower WTP than respondents outside the BRM. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Secondly, we make an initial investigation into the existence of a distance decay effect on the status quo (i.e. the choice of no project) on the sub-sample of respondents inside BRM. One hypothesis to be tested was that the further individuals are from the Rupt de Mad watershed, the less concerned they are about improving environmental quality. We calculated distances (denoted dist) using the centroid of the respondent's municipality of residence (Farber and Griner, [2000]; Campbell et al. [2008, 2009]; Rolfe and Windle [2012]; Johnston and Ramachandran [2014]) and considered straight line distances to the nearest point of the Rupt de Mad watershed, as this may be appropriate for assessing NUVs where access to the site of interest is not required (De Valck et al. [2017]; Holland and Johnston [2017]). After excluding two respondents living in proximity to the watershed, the main econometric estimates concerned 627 individuals (i.e., 15 048 observations = 627 × 3 alternatives × 8 choice sets). We introduce the variable dist and its squared term dist 2. Both variables are significantly different from zero in models (2) and (4). The negative sign of the parameter of dist indicates that people further away from the study site reject the 'no project' alternative. The positive effect of dist^2 means that this effect is non-linear and decreases with distance. [SOURCE: Page_148]

146 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Focusing on the MXL results based on the sample of respondents within the BRM, we can first note that all the standard deviations associated with the mean coefficients of the random variables are significantly different from zero, indicating the existence of a strong heterogeneity of preferences for the different environmental attributes. [SOURCE: Page_150]

The parameter ASC associated with the SQ, i.e. the 'no project' alternative, is significantly negative, implying that respondents preferred to choose a project rather than the SQ. As expected, the coefficients associated with the attributes "ban on pesticides", "restoration of watercourse" and "no negative effect of fertilizer" are positive (all highly significantly different from zero). The overall negative effect on water quality resulting from the use of both organic and chemical fertilizers is rejected by individuals. Hedgerow maintenance and hedgerow planting are strongly preferred to hedgerow clearing without the project. Finally, forest management that favours biodiversity (in addition to timber production and water conservation) is valued by the respondents. [SOURCE: Page_150]

We then calculated individual WTP for environmental attributes using the individual-level parameters corresponding to the attribute variables and the (constant) monetary attribute parameter (Revelt and Train [2000]; Train [2009], chapter 11) from the MXL results (Table 5, column 4). The WTPs are based on the monetary attribute included in the DCE, i.e. the increase in the annual water bill used to finance environmental projects. [SOURCE: Page_150]

148 [SOURCE: Page_150]

Table 2-6 : Mean WTP for each attribute by distance category [SOURCE: Page_151]

Table 2.7 shows the individual WTP regression results for the seven different environmental attributes of our CE. These allow us to pre-test different hypotheses, such as the existence of distance decay (see Table 2.6) and the effect of individual characteristics, and in particular to know whether bottled water drinkers and non-drinkers have different WTP for these attributes. For example, we find that relative to the “always” drinker of bottled water the occasional drinkers seem to have a higher WTP for water course restoration, no negative effect of fertilizers and forest biodiversity measures. [SOURCE: Page_151]

### 4.2.         Latent class analysis

In addition to the CL and MLX models, we decided to estimate a latent class model (LCM) in order to have more information about the heterogeneity of the attributes (Table 2.8). The LCM can be seen as an extension of the MLX with one notable difference (Greene and Hensher [2003]). Whereas the MLX accounts for preference heterogeneity by allowing utility parameters to vary randomly (and continuously) across individuals, the LCM classifies individuals into a (discrete) number of classes, with a probability of belonging to a particular class. The number of classes is determined based on information criteria, such as Bayesian Information Criteria (BIC) and Akaike Information Criteria (AIC). A comparison between MLX and LCM can be found in Greene and Hensher [2003], and applications to environmental issues in Neves do Prado and Abildtrup [2022] and Schaak and Musshoff [2019], among others. [SOURCE: Page_153]

We therefore analyse respondents' choices using a four-class LCM. Based on the determinants identified for individual WTP, we tested different individual characteristics to include in the part of the model that explains the probability of belonging to a particular class. These characteristics are distance categories, age categories, level and type of bottled water drinker. We selected the preferred number of classes by repeatedly estimating the LCM with different numbers of classes, optimising the BIC and AIC. [SOURCE: Page_153]

By comparing the BIC and AIC during the repeated rounds of LCM estimation, we did not find an optimal criterion, but rather that the change in BIC and AIC decreased significantly between classes four and five (with no convergence for six classes), and we therefore decided that a four-classes model would be most appropriate for analysing respondents' choices. On these four classes, one attracts a majority of respondents (55%) and the other three are more or less equal, ranging from 12% to 16.7%. Estimation results of the LCM are presented in Table 2.8. Furthermore, Table 2.9 presents estimates of marginal WTP associated with each environmental attribute along with 95% confidence intervals computed with Krinsky and Robb [1986] method.22 [SOURCE: Page_153]

22 Although the delta method is the most common method for calculating standard errors of partial effects, it uses a first-order Taylor series expansion of a nonlinear function of parameters evaluated at a given point of approximation, which may not be accurate (Krinsky and Robb [1986]; Dowd et al. [2014]. [SOURCE: Page_153]

impacts on water quality and biodiversity (significant only at the 10% level). Respondents with a lower level of education and those who drink bottled water more than occasionally are more likely to belong to this pro-status quo class. [SOURCE: Page_155]

Class 2 is that with a negative but small coefficient associated with the monetary attribute, and only at the 10% level. Individuals in this class show strong opposition to the status quo and clear preferences for environmental attributes such as no pesticides, water restoration, hedgerow planting or forest management that favours timber production, water quality and biodiversity conservation. However, their marginal WTP for all attributes is not significantly different from zero at the 5% level (see Table 2.9). WTP is only significantly different from zero at the 10% level^23 and only for the absence of pesticides, water restoration, hedgerow planting and forest management that benefits the environment. [SOURCE: Page_155]

Class 3 is only concerned with the pesticide attribute, but with a strong preference (and a mean value of €133.72, see Table 9). This class has a predicted share of 16.7% of the sample. We can see that the respondents who are not in this class are the furthest away from the site (dist_cat^200). [SOURCE: Page_155]

Finally, class 4 includes people with a high and significantly negative coefficient for the monetary attribute. They only have a significant preference for banning pesticides and restoring watercourses (at the 5% level) and are willing to pay a low amount. [SOURCE: Page_155]
