### 5. Discussion and conclusion

This paper analyzes the NUV of water and biodiversity protection in a watershed in eastern France. While measures to protect or improve water quality are usually considered to generate direct use values for water consumers, we show that non-water consumers may also have positive preferences for measures that have positive impacts on water quality and biodiversity. Our latent class analysis of our DCE data, produced quite contrasting results. [SOURCE: Page_156]

First, different classes of respondents were identified. The first (Class 1), whose respondents are highly and significantly opposed to the environmental project. However, they are willing to pay a significant amount (€10.69) per year on the water bill to ban pesticides. The second (Class 2), for which the monetary attribute has little significant effect on their choice and for which their WTP (for banning pesticides, restoring watercourses, planting hedges and managing forests) is high but estimated with a large confidence interval, due to weakly significant coefficient on the monetary attribue. Class 3, whose members are closest to the site and who are willing to pay a very high amount (€133.72) for pesticides ban. Class 4 have only positive preferences for in pesticide ban and watercourse restoration and are only willing to pay low amounts (€3.48 and €4.60, respectively). [SOURCE: Page_156]

Although they capture a large amount of heterogeneity between people, these results can be compared with previous studies found in the literature. In France, several studies have investigated WTP for the preservation of groundwater quality (Hérivaux and Rinaudo [2016]; Rinaudo and Aulong [2014]; Hérivaux [2011]; Rinaudo [2008]; Grappey [1999] Stenger and Willinger [1995], Rozan et al. [1996]). For example, Stenger and Willinger [1995] used CVM to calculate water users' conservation values for the Alsatian aquifer threatened by various pollutants (nitrates, herbicides, mercury). They found that the average conservation value was about 700FF/household/year (about €110 in 1996). This estimate was extended to non-users (Rozan et al. [1996]). These non-users were potential future users as they were located close to the aquifer and the quality of their current water resource was also threatened by pollution. This second study showed that the WTP of the non-users was 60% of the conservation value of the users). [SOURCE: Page_156]

Hérivaux [2011] investigated public perception and WTP for groundwater quality improvement in a part of the Meuse alluvial aquifer near Liège (Belgium). Even though the [SOURCE: Page_156]

154 [SOURCE: Page_156]

proportion of water users was close to zero (1.5%), the author emphasised that people living above the aquifer were more likely to contribute to groundwater quality improvement (72% compared to 62% for the others), and WTP amounts were likely to be higher for households living closer to the Meuse. Nevertheless, the main motivation for contributing to the protection measures was the possibility of passing on a better quality groundwater body to future generations. In monetary terms, the average WTP is 46.9 €/household/year if only positive WT are considered and 40.1 €/household/year if real zeros are included. [SOURCE: Page_157]

Interestingly, some studies focused on areas close to our area of interest: Hérivaux and Rinaudo [2016] and Rinaudo [2008], conducted a study in eastern France (Meurthe et Moselle and Vosges), where the Lower Triassic sandstone aquifer is located. In contrast to other studies presented here, the aquifer was facing over-exploitation rather than pollution. Nevertheless, the study provides interesting information: firstly, if most respondents used this water for recreational or other purposes, the main motivation to pay was related to non-use (transfer of quality drinking water to future generations), confirming Rinaudo and Aulong [2014]. In this case, respondents had a higher WTP than others. In addition, the binary variable indicating respondents' agreement with the statement "I am willing to pay for this aquifer as I would be willing to pay for any other aquifer in France" was significant. The average WTP was 39.5€/household/year if true zeros were included and 45€/household/year if they were not. [SOURCE: Page_157]

We also investigated a possible distance decay effect on non-users' preferences for environmental attributes. However, the results are moderate and seem to indicate that distance matters only for a small proportion of respondents (allocated in Class 3). [SOURCE: Page_157]

We hypothesised that the respondents' water consumption habits would influence the results, i.e. if you buy bottled water, you might be very concerned about water quality and therefore have strong preferences for water conservation measures, even if you do not consume the protected groundwater. We did not find a clear effect of consuming bottled water. This may be due to different effects. For example, some people may consume bottled water out of convenience and not because they are concerned about water quality, or some respondents may choose not to consume bottled water to reduce resource use because they are concerned about the environment, which may correlate with preferences for water protection measures. Future studies should look more closely at the factors that determine preferences for water quality and protection. The fact that drinking water protection measures can have significant [SOURCE: Page_157]

155 [SOURCE: Page_157]

NUVs has important implications for the regulation of water resources. In particular, it could justify the French delegation of water and biodiversity protection to the regional water agencies, as it allows for a more comprehensive spatial consideration and the integration of water and biodiversity concerns in the regulation, although not all measures have the same value. In fact, water protection measures include NUVs that are not only related to the quality of water for consumption, and we find that it is not only local residents who benefit from protection measures. Some inhabitants living far from the Rupt de Mad water catchment area (even more than 100 km) have a positive WTP for some protection measures, although this is mostly limited to the ban on pesticides and the restoration of watercourses. [SOURCE: Page_158]

We found that all respondents considered a pesticides ban to be an important environmental measure, complementing the findings of Amiri et al. [2022] who found an important utility value of pesticides bans in recreational areas. Therefore, French water authorities should prioritize pesticides bans as a measure to protect water and biodiversity, in order to be in line with public preferences. In this paper, we have assumed that the WTP for water protection of individuals who do not consume water from the protected catchment area in the scenarios proposed in the DCE reflect NUVs. This is based on the assumption that respondents did not consider that they would one day benefit from the improved water quality, for example by moving to the area served by the protected resources. However, we believe that this assumption will generally not be violated as very few will have this perception. Furthermore, even if the assumption is violated, our results still show that the value of water protection does not only benefit current drinking water consumers. Similarly, we assume that the respondents are not (future) recreational users of the watershed. However, the lack of a strong distance decay effect suggests that respondents did not consider the catchment as a potential recreational site. [SOURCE: Page_158]

156 [SOURCE: Page_158]

12)   Nous souhaitons évaluer la valeur que vous accordez aux actions de protections des ressources en eau et de la biodiversité (interdiction d'utiliser des pesticides, renaturation de cours d'eau etc.) mises en place par l'agence de l'eau Rhin-Meuse sur un territoire spécifique (zone délimitée par le liseré rouge sur la carte). Ce territoire, de 385 km², est le bassin versant du Rupt de Mad, un cours d'eau de 55 km, qui contribue à hauteur d'environ 70 % à l’alimentation en eau de la ville de Metz et alimente également une quinzaine de communes aux alentours. Les mesures mises en place améliorent la qualité des eaux brutes (eaux de surface et eaux souterraines), diminuant ainsi les coûts de traitement de l'eau pour toutes les communes en question. Pour en savoir plus sur le contexte dans lequel s'inscrivent ces actions, passez à la question suivante. [SOURCE: Page_163]

161 [SOURCE: Page_163]

13) Les agences de l’eau sont des établissements publics ayant pour mission de gérer et de préserver la ressource en eau, les milieux aquatiques et la biodiversité. Dans ce but, elles participent au financement de projets, (comme, par exemple, la renaturation de cours d'eau) et accompagnent différents acteurs (collectivités, industriels, agriculteurs) afin de promouvoir des pratiques plus favorables à la qualité de l'eau (utilisation moins intensive de pesticides etc.). [SOURCE: Page_164]

Nous allons vous présenter, au cours des questions suivantes, différents projets de préservation de la qualité de l'eau et de la biodiversité. Vous devrez alors choisir, selon vos préférences entre le projet A, le projet B et l'option "absence de projet". Ces projets possèdent des caractéristiques (renaturation de cours d'eau, modalités de gestion de la forêt etc.) qui pourront prendre des "niveaux" différents: le projet A pourra, par exemple, prévoir la renaturation d'un cours d'eau, contrairement au projet B. Chaque niveau sera illustré par un pictogramme. [SOURCE: Page_164]

162 [SOURCE: Page_164]

Nous partons du principe que toutes les actions prévues au cours d'un projet seront effectivement mises en œuvre et que votre contribution financière sera uniquement utilisée pour mener ce projet à bien. Gardez néanmoins à l'esprit que si vous décidez de choisir un projet, vous et votre foyer auront en conséquence moins d'argent à allouer à d'autres usages. Après avoir pris connaissance des caractéristiques des projets (présentées ci-dessous), passez directement à la page suivante: nous vous détaillerons la marche à suivre avec un exemple. ### 1. Introduction

The attractiveness of rural areas and how to improve it has been questioned for a long time in France (Aznar, 2002; CREDOC, 2001). The identification of the drivers of local development is key for the design of appropriate regional policies, especially in terms of territorial planning (Berriet-Solliec et al. 2022; Aznar, 2002; Poncet and Belot, 1991). Since 1999, in France, the rural development policy has been officially recognized as the "second pillar" of the Common Agricultural Policy 24 (CAP) (Pham and Berriet-Solliec, 2018) and is supported by the programme hexagonal de développement rural (PHDR). This program, was, divided in several axes (until 2007) and then priorities, linked with various aspects of rural development: improving the competitiveness of agriculture and forestry, preserving or restoring ecosystems and biodiversity or improving the quality of life in rural areas (Pham and Berriet-Solliec, 2018; Allaire et al. 2018). Some of these objectives are more or less directly linked with the residential economy (Berriet-Solliec et al. 2022), which is an important issue in the rural world: since the period 1970-1980, the rural population is growing (MTECT, 2022; Perrier- Cornet, 2017; Bertrand, 2006; Perrier-Cornet, 2003; Perrier-Cornet and Soulard, 2003). However, that trend is not uniform: rural communities near cities benefit from urban sprawling and periurbanization dynamics, when other rural territories remain isolated (Perrier-Cornet, 2017; Bertrand, 2006; Perrier-Cornet and Soulard, 2003; Perrier-Cornet, 2003; Béteille, 1981). In this context, the valorization of territorial amenities can be key in rural development (MASA; 2021; Doré, 2008). The role of these amenities in the growth of the residential economy refers to the countryside as « cadre de vie » (Aznar, 2002; Perrier- Cornet, 2002). More broadly, rural economic structures face a shift from a development based on natural resources extraction (from mining and agriculture for instance) to a growth that relies on environmental quality (through tourism, for example) (Naranpanawa, 2019; Power, 1996). [SOURCE: Page_183]

In France, the development of the residential sphere (sometimes at the expense of agricultural land use) led to a shift in research programs, following Brun et al. (1992) and Cavailhès et al. (1994), less focused on agricultural economics and more on spatial, regional and urban [SOURCE: Page_183]

24 This     "second         pillar"       of       the        CAP         is       presented   here: https://www.europarl.europa.eu/factsheets/fr/sheet/110/second-pilier-de-la-pac-la-politique-de- developpement-rural [SOURCE: Page_183]

181 [SOURCE: Page_183]

economics: the microeconomic determinants of households' residential choices is one of the key issues (Aubert and Schmitt, 2014). Environmental amenities appear to be one of them. These amenities (landscape, climate etc.) can, in return, be degraded by residents and tourists (Legras, 2016). [SOURCE: Page_184]

However, originally, the term “amenities'' doesn’t originally refer to the environment (MASA, 2021; Mollard et al. 2014). Economists started to use it in reference to nature 25 with the rise of environmental concerns in the 1970’s-1980’s (Mollard et al. 2014): Power (1996; 1988; 1980), started to define the theoretical framework on why amenities play an important role in rural economic growth patterns, alongside with Marcouiller (1998) and Marcouiller and Clendenning (2004). The notion even got into economic institutions with reports from OECD (2001; 1999; 1996; 1994). Finally, Power (2005) and Marcouiller and Clendenning (2004) discussed supply and demand from a theoretical perspective. These amenities are often public goods that are not traded on any market. To estimate their value, environmental economists have developed a range of nonmarket valuation, including the hedonic price method (HPM) (Lautrup et al. 2023; Osland et al. 2022; Power, 2005). Application to rural areas can be challenging^26: the literature suggests that studies involving urban zones are more likely to identify significant amenity effects (Waltert and Schläpfer, 2010). Le Goffe (1996) pointed out that applying hedonics to agricultural territories implies to consider wide geographic scales, in order to take into account variability in environmental effects from farming. Another issue concerns data availability and quality: first, data concerning the real estate market in rural areas has not always been easily accessible or even reliable (Michalland and Volet, 1999). Second, finding data about some specific environmental attributes (water quality, biodiversity, landscape etc.) that can influence housing prices can be challenging as well (Le Goffe, 1996). As a consequence, the HPM seemed to be historically most applied in urban contexts (White and Leefers, 2007; Le Goffe; 1996). Nevertheless, over the years, substantial improvements have been made for hedonic characteristics (Casanova et al. 2017) as well as for environmental data^27 availability about rural territories. [SOURCE: Page_184]

25 Other amenities were also considered: see Power (2005). 26 The reasons why the hedonic price method is difficult to apply in rural areas are summed up in Michalland and Vollet (1999). 27 The creation of the rural                development      observatory   is   a   good   example:   see https://odr.inrae.fr/intranet/carto_joomla/ [SOURCE: Page_184]

182 [SOURCE: Page_184]

Rosen (1974) established the hedonic theoretical framework in the 1970’s, but the idea of hedonic pricing can be traced back in the 1920-1930 decade (Court, 1939; Waugh, 1929). Concerning environmental amenities, early thoughts seemed to be about the role of climate on migration (Ullman; 1954; Bright and Thomas, 1941). After Rosen's seminal paper, the number of contributions using hedonics to value environmental amenities (or disamenities) quickly started to grow (Freeman, 1981). In France, one of the first applications was made by Verney (1976), who used property price models to assess the cost of nuisance from solid waste treatment units. Le Goffe and Delache (1997) and Le Goffe (2000) were among the firsts to apply it to the french rural context. The HPM has been applied to various topics related to the countryside: many studies investigate the impact of agriculture on rural tourism (Mollard et al. 2007; Vanslembrouck et al. 2005; Le Goffe, 2000; Le Goffe and Delache, 1997) or on residential properties (Osseni et al. 2019; Ready and Abdalla, 2005). The method is also useful to assess the impact of rural policies (Liljenstolpe, 2011). Some studies focus on peri-urban areas (Jensen et al. 2021; Sylla et al. 2019). Nature based solutions (NBS) (Bockarjowa and Botzen, 2017) and environmental management in general (Chen et al. 2022) can, through ecosystem services provision or preservation, have a substantial impact on residential prices, and participate in rural and peri-urban zones’ attractivity. However, these spillovers are often unequally distributed and must be measured using spatial hedonic analysis. (Hu et al. 2022; Osland et al. 2020; Maslianskaïa-Pautrel and Baumont, 2016). The most common strategy is to measure the distance between residential properties and the nearest environmental amenitiy of each category, using geographic information systems (GIS). But as Maslianskaïa-Pautrel and Baumont (2016) emphasized on the fact that the price of a property is not only affected by environmental features located in one place, but also by the environmental endowments on neighboring places, measures of spatial domination of the characteristics in the area surrounding each building (see for instance Kong, et al. 2007), and vista measures regarding the oceanic view from each locations (Fu et al. 2019; Hamilton & Morgan, 2010) can be used. Very few studies, with the exception of Cavailhès et al. (2009), however, have been combining all three types of methods (distance, domination and vista measures). [SOURCE: Page_185]

183 [SOURCE: Page_185]

That (reciprocal) effect of amenity improvement between neighboring housing induce a spatial multiplier effect: the direct effect of the improvement is amplified by the spillover 28 effects among surrounding properties (Maslianskaïa-Pautrel and Baumont, 2016b; Small and Steimetz, 2012). This multiplier is given by the coefficient of a weighted transformation of spatially lagged prices (Small and Steimetz, 2012). [SOURCE: Page_186]

Moreover, some authors notice the existence of diffusion effects of properties’ prices (home buyers and sellers may use information from the nearest sales to determine their buying and selling prices) or of environmental externalities (an externality may impact individuals who live in other locations) that need to be addressed (Osseni et al. 2019). [SOURCE: Page_186]

In a nutshell, when conducting an hedonic valuation two types of spatial effects have to be controlled for: a) the spatial dependencies potentially present in the variables – spatial autocorrelation and spatial heterogeneity – b) the impact of spatial processes – spatial diffusion and the spatial multiplier phenomenons (Maslianskaïa-Pautrel and Baumont, 2016b). [SOURCE: Page_186]

In this article, we want to investigate the influence of agri-environmental measures related to a payment for environmental services scheme implemented in les Vosges (France). The private company, Nestlé Waters, that owns several mineral natural or spring waters’ brands, such as Vittel®, Hépar®, Contrex® etc. had to cope, in the end of the 1980’s, with a deterioration of the water quality, due to nitrates’ pollution resulting from intensive farming (Amiri et al. 2022; Déprés et al. 2005). To address this issue, Agrivair (a subsidiary of Nestlé Waters dedicated to these ecological issues) started to collaborate with the French National Institute of Agronomic Research (INRA) and local farmers. Some concrete measures were adopted to guarantee water quality: cessation of corn culture, composting of animal waste, plantation of trees etc. Moreover, permanent grasslands cannot be turned over anymore. The Vittel case became the « first recognized initiative » of Payment for Environmental Services (PES) in France (Hernandez and Benoit, 2011). This PES therefore results in land use modifications that may be reflected in house prices. Previous hedonic studies have been conducted to examine the effect of nature-based solutions in an urban setting (Bockarjova and Botzen, 2017), or the impact rural policies on real estate prices (Liljenstolpe, 2011): this study contributes to the literature by assessing the influence of a PES scheme on such prices in a [SOURCE: Page_186]

28 Small and Steimetz (2012) distinguish pecuniary spillovers from technological ones. [SOURCE: Page_186]

184 [SOURCE: Page_186]

rural area: this kind of valuation is crucial in a context where the role of amenities is at the heart of economic rural development (MASA, 2021). [SOURCE: Page_187]

### 2.1.     Analytical framework

The HPM has been widely used in environmental economics. Following Rosen (1974), this technique based on revealed preferences aims at determining the marginal WTP for characteristics of a composite good (Osland et al. 2020; Osseni et al. 2019). The housing market is considered as appropriate, because the house and apartments prices are assumed to be a function of the capitalized values of all the attributes that can influence the housing value (house characteristics or environmental amenities) (Łaszkiewicz et al. 2022; Osland et al. 2020, Osseni et al. 2019). [SOURCE: Page_187]

Hedonic analysis consists in two related steps often referred to as a first-stage and second- stage analysis (Taylor, 2017). In a first-stage analysis, the hedonic price function is estimated using information about the sales prices of a differentiated product and its characteristics. Doing so, researchers are able to recover the implicit prices of these attributes and to elicit individuals’s preferences for it, by estimating the willingness to pay (WTP) for environmental variables. These informations can then be combined with data on household characteristics to estimate demand functions for the characteristics or utility parameters (Marrouch and Sayour, 2021; Taylor, 2017). Accounting for spatial dependence of environmental amenities can be seen as a third step (Marrouch and Sayour, 2021). The literature seems mostly restricted to first stage analyses, mainly because second-stage one is data demanding and can generate endogeneity issues (Jensen et al. 2021; Taylor, 2017). Possible endogeneity sources and strategies to control for this biais are discussed by Osseni et al. (2021) [SOURCE: Page_187]

Let's assume Z represents a product with Z$_1$, Z$_2$, Zn characteristics. Z is assumed to be sold in a perfectly competitive market, and its equilibrium price is P(ẕ). Consumer utility is defined over two goods: Z, and x, a composite product representing all other goods. The utility function of a consumer J is defined as : [SOURCE: Page_187]

Uj = (x, z^1, z^2, … , zn ; αj)                                                                 (1) [SOURCE: Page_187]

185 [SOURCE: Page_187]

The marginal rate of substitution between any characteristic zi, and x, is equal to the rate at which the consumer can trade zi for x in the market: [SOURCE: Page_188]

∂P        ∂u/∂zi                                                                              (2) = ∂zi       ∂U/∂x [SOURCE: Page_188]

Besides these theoretical considerations, researchers have to decide what functional form of hedonic price function to consider. Little theoretical guidance exists for the choice of this functional form (Taylor, 2017) but non-linear ones appear to be more relevant (Bishop et al. 2020; Kuminoff et al. 2010; Ekeland et al. 2004). Even if Cropper et al. (1988) stated that simpler forms (such as linear, log-linear, or log-log) perform better when dealing with omitted variable bias, Kuminoff et al. (2010) challenged this finding: the found that including spatial fixed effects to control for omitted variables can solve this problem. In that case, more flexible specifications (such as quadratic Box-Cox) outperform other models. Moreover, nonlinear forms also allow the market equilibrium to reflect complementarities between amenities (Albouy et al. 2020). The log-log functional form seems to be the most frequently used in hedonic studies (Maslianskaïa-Pautrel and Baumont, 2016b). Aside from that, researchers must be careful about heteroskedasticity and (spatial and temporal) autocorrelation of standard errors (Bishop et al. 2020). [SOURCE: Page_188]

### 2.2.1. Specification

The traditional hedonic price model has been widely used to study price variations between different residential properties. According to our analysis framework, a general model could be represented as following: [SOURCE: Page_188]

f(Pi, Xi, Ai, εi) = 0                                                                        (3) [SOURCE: Page_188]

Where where 𝑃𝑖 is the value of residential property 𝑖, 𝑋𝑖 is a vector of house characteristics, 𝐴𝑖 is a vector of variables representing amenities, 𝜀𝑖 is an independent and identically distributed error term. [SOURCE: Page_188]

186 [SOURCE: Page_188]

Economic theory does not specify which variables should be included in a hedonic regression, nor does it specify the functional form. The choice of functional form is an important empirical issue. There are many basic functional forms (e.g. linear, quadratic, log-linear, log- log) that can be applied to the hedonic price model. However, we find little guidance on the functional forms to be used, except that the price of a property is likely to vary non-linearly with some housing or location characteristics. [SOURCE: Page_189]

The first, simplest functional form is the linear form. The advantage is that the marginal effect directly expresses the implicit marginal price of an additional unit of the attribute: [SOURCE: Page_189]

Pi = β0 + βXXi + βAAi + εi                                                                                 (4) [SOURCE: Page_189]

A more general functional form that introduces non-linearity and thus more flexibility would be one where the explained variable is log-transformed and the list of explanatory variables could include linear, quadratic and logarithmic transformations of dwelling characteristics: [SOURCE: Page_189]

ln(𝑃𝑖) = 𝛽0 + 𝛽𝑋 ln(𝑋𝑖) + 𝛽𝐴𝐴𝑖 + 𝛽𝐴𝐴2𝑖 + 𝛽𝐷𝐷𝑖 + 𝛽𝐴𝐷𝐴𝑖 × 𝐷𝑖 + 𝜀𝑖                                            (5) [SOURCE: Page_189]

where 𝐷𝑖 could be a dummy variable. [SOURCE: Page_189]

In the latter case, we use a Poisson regression model to estimate the positive value of the dependent variable (Cameron and Trivedi, 2005). We prefer a Poisson model to a log- transformed model because OLS-estimated parameters of the latter lead to biased estimates in the presence of heteroskedasticity (Manning and Mullahy, 2001; Santos Silva and Tenreyro, 2006).29 [SOURCE: Page_189]

From the equation (5), the Poisson regression can be written as: [SOURCE: Page_189]

E(𝑃𝑖|𝑋𝑖, 𝐴𝑖, 𝐷𝑖) = exp[𝛽0 + 𝛽𝑋 ln(𝑋𝑖) + 𝛽𝐴𝐴𝑖 + 𝛽𝐴𝐴2𝑖 + 𝛽𝐷𝐷𝑖 + 𝛽𝐴𝐷𝐴𝑖 × 𝐷𝑖]                                   (6) [SOURCE: Page_189]

where 𝑃𝑖 does not follow a specific distribution. [SOURCE: Page_189]

29 Poisson regression is well known for dealing with count data. However, it is also an increasingly used specification for estimating multiplicative models where the dependent variable is non-negative, see for example its use for estimating a gravity equation in international trade. [SOURCE: Page_189]

187 [SOURCE: Page_189]

### 2.2.2. Estimation procedure

In our case of the hedonic price model, we are likely to have many omitted variables that are a source of unobserved heterogeneity, leading to potential endogeneity bias. We could control for these issues by introducing various fixed effects. In particular, we should control for municipality specific effects γj and time effects λt. We can rewrite the error term as consisting of several components: [SOURCE: Page_190]

εijt = γj + λt + ϵijt                                                                                    (7) [SOURCE: Page_190]

where 𝜖𝑖𝑗𝑡 is the idiosyncratic error term. It is worth noting that there is no individual specific effect as we do not have a typical panel structure in the sense that the transaction for a dwelling is realised only once. [SOURCE: Page_190]

The estimation method used is the Poisson pseudo-maximum likelihood (PPML) implemented by the Stata command ppmlhdfe (Correia et al., 2020). [SOURCE: Page_190]

### 3.1. Case study

In this article, we want to investigate the influence of agri-environmental measures related to a payment for environmental services scheme implemented in les Vosges (France), a french, mostly rural, department, in the Grand-Est region (table 3.1). The rural/urban distinction is given by the national institute of statistics and economic studies (INSEE), with the following classification^30: [SOURCE: Page_190]

1) Rural municipalities out of the influence of an employment hub (pôle d’emploi, in french) also called autonomous rural municipalities; [SOURCE: Page_190]

2) Rural municipalities under the influence of an employment hub (i.e within an attraction area of more than 500 000 inhabitants), also called rural periurban municipalities. [SOURCE: Page_190]

30 see https://www.insee.fr/fr/statistiques/5359123#consulter for additional classification details. [SOURCE: Page_190]

188 [SOURCE: Page_190]

Grand-Est                       Autonomous                   Rural      Autonomous +             Urban departments                         rural                   periurban      periurban           municipalities municipalities             municipal    municipalities            (%) (%)                    ities (%) (%) [SOURCE: Page_191]

Meuse                                       52.8                        21.5       74.3                   25.7 Haute-Marne                                 34.1                        33.2       67.3                   32.7 Vosges                                      40.1                        27.3       67.4                   32.7 Ardennes                                    28.2                        26         54.2                   45.8 Aube                                        22.3                        26.4       48.7                   51.4 Marne                                       17.9                        24.4       42.3                   57.7 Moselle                                     11.1                        22.5       33.6                   66.5 Meurthe-et-Moselle                           9.1                        21         30.1                   69.9 Bas-Rhin                                    11.4                        18.4       29.8                   70.3 Haut-Rhin                                    5.1                        23.3       28.4                   71.6 Grand-Est                                   16.2                        22.8        39                     61 Source: INSEE, population census (2018) [SOURCE: Page_191]

Table 3-1 : Share of rural/urban municipalities in Grand-Est departments [SOURCE: Page_191]

The Vosges are among the most rural departments in the Grand-Est region. In addition, a lot of municipalities are even classified in “zone de revitalisation rurale” (ZRR) (figure 3.1), which gather territories sharing some socio-economic characteristics, such as low population density, declining total (or working) population or a high proportion of agricultural jobs. The Vittel’s watershed (or impluvium) (in blue) is located right in the biggest ZRR (in red), in the west of the department. Other municipalities are in white. Two-third of the territory belongs to that category (338 municipalities over 507). [SOURCE: Page_191]

189 [SOURCE: Page_191]

Figure 3-1: “Zones de revitalisation rurale” in les Vosges (source: authors) [SOURCE: Page_192]

It is more than the mean of the Grand-Est region^31 (52.3%) but less than in Meuse (94.6%), Haute-Marne (90.1%) or Ardennes (83.1%). Considering the rural character of the department, we may expect houses' location to be close to agricultural fields: Guilpart et al. (2022) have shown that the dominant land use at 150 m to residential buildings in the Vosges is grasslands (both permanent and temporary), as it is the case at the national level. We want to test the influence of that land use on hedonic pricing compared to other ones, such as agriculture or forest. [SOURCE: Page_192]

The evolution of grasslands in the Vosges is downward but rather stable^32 over the years. In the impluvium, the share of permanent grasslands (PG) is 56% but the situation vary between farmers: as turning over PG is prohibited for those who have signed PSE contracts, the share of PG among these farms is 65% against 41% for non-signatories farmers (Agrivair data, [SOURCE: Page_192]

31 See: https://www.observatoire-des-territoires.gouv.fr/kiosque/zonage-les-zones-de-revitalisation-rurale-zrr 32 See: https://grandest.chambre-agriculture.fr/fileadmin/user_upload/Grand-Est/049_Inst-Acal/RUBR-agro- environnement/Changement_climatique/8_0_ORACLE_Grand_Est_2020_theme_attenuation_agricole.pdf [SOURCE: Page_192]

190 [SOURCE: Page_192]

2020). Older data about land use in the impluvium, especially grasslands and grasslands reversal are available in Roussel (1999), Deffontaines et al. (1993) and Gaury (1992). Deffontaines et al. (1993) indicate that historically, the maximum surface covered by grasslands has been reached in the 1970’s. The table 3.2 sum up the evolution of grasslands on the impluvium before the PES implementation: [SOURCE: Page_193]

Year                                  1970                      1979               1991        Evolution 1970-1991 (%) Surface of permanent                  2058                      1789               1507           - 26.77 grasslands (ha) Surface of temporary                     6                       19                200             + 3233 grasslands (ha) Total (ha)                            2064                      1808               1707            -17.3 Adapted from Gaury (1992) Table 3-2 : Evolution of grassland share in the impluvium before PES implementation [SOURCE: Page_193]

In comparison the “surface toujours en herbe” represented almost 75% % of the useful agricultural area in the Vosges department in 1970, a very stable percentage until 1988 (Fiorelli, 1998). [SOURCE: Page_193]

Meadows are known to be a favorable land use for water quality (Rhin-Meuse basin committee 2021; Peyraud et al. 2012; Salles et al. 2009), but they produce many other ecosystem services, such as carbon sequestration, soil erosion limitation and biodiversity reserve (Rhin-Meuse basin committee 2021; Peyraud et al. 2012; Salles et al. 2009). The influence on both permanent and temporary grasslands on ecosystem services can vary according to management practices or fertilization levels. For temporary grasslands, it also depends on how often the meadow is turned (Thiébaud et al. 2001). As Le Goffe and Delache (1997), we make the hypothesis that grasslands (permanent grasslands in particular), symbolize more extensive agricultural systems, and may have a positive impact on house prices. Besides, it may represent a sign of landscape openness, especially in areas dominated by forests. Past evidences in the literature are quite mixed: Le Goffe and Delache (1997) found a positive but non significant effect of permanent meadows on rural cottages in [SOURCE: Page_193]

191 [SOURCE: Page_193]

Brittany, while Vanslembrouck et al. (2005) found a significant effect for the same kind of study in Belgium. Osseni et al. (2019) investigated the effect of both temporary and permanent grasslands in Brittany at different spatial scales. They found a negative effect of both temporary and permanent grasslands at a very local scale, but positive at a larger one. In general, this land uses seems to have a rather positive but not always significant effect on prices like in Osseni et al. (2021). Nevertheless, exceptions can be found in Gibbons et al. (2014) and Liljenstolpe (2011). [SOURCE: Page_194]

Informations about meadows (and other land uses) come from the Corine Land Cover database, a biophysical land use inventory that maps homogeneous units of land use (or land use change) with a minimum surface area of 25 ha. Based on this nomenclature, we distinguished five different land uses: meadows, forest, agriculture, urban zones and others. The Vosges are dominated by forest areas, mainly divided between broad-leaved forests (23.11%) and coniferous forests (21.02%). In our analysis, we separated grasslands (26.51%) from lawns and natural pastures (0.27%), often located in accidental areas, and that can include rock surfaces and brambles. Details about our classification are available in annex 1. Concerning agriculture, the two main categories include non-irrigated arable land (11.79%) (mainly cereals, pulses and fodder crops) and complex cultivation patterns (4.23%). [SOURCE: Page_194]

Data are available for different years: 1990, 2000, 2006, 2012 and 2018. Because Agrivair, the structure in charge of the implementation of agri-environmental measures on the impluvium was created in 1992, we could have considered data from 2000 to 2018 to measure the effect of such measures on house prices. But as land use shares have not drastically changed over time in the Vosges (table 3.3), we focused on the 2012 year as a preliminary analysis. [SOURCE: Page_194]

Land                     2000               2006         2012    2018        Evolution uses/Year                                                                     2000-2018 Meadows                21.12              27.67        26.51   25.82      + 22.25 Forest               50.31             48.99         49.80   51.37       +2.11 Agriculture             20.56             15.80         16.22   15.45       -24.85 Urban                3.25                3.11        3.21    3.62       +11.38 Others               4.76                4.43        4.26     3.74       -21.43 Total               100                 100         100      100 Table 3-3 : Evolution of land use in les Vosges (2000-2018)              Source: Corine Land Cover [SOURCE: Page_194]

The most important evolution of land use concerns agricultural plots, but still remains limited. The relative stability of the meadows share may be explained by the supportive policies implemented from 1970’s in France in order to maintain grassland areas (Institut de l’élevage, 2007). More generally, land use evolutions are not so important over the period (2000-2018) and considering one specific year may not lead to important changes in the results. [SOURCE: Page_195]

Our hedonic analysis is conducted using the Perval database (Notaires de France). Our transaction data covers the period 1998-2021 for the Vosges department, with 40 650 observations before cleaning. First, we removed observations with missing geographic coordinates (293 observations) and irrelevant ones (actually out of the Vosges). It led us to 40 316 observations. Then, we also removed houses located in the Vosges mountains, because touristic accommodation is highly represented in the area (INSEE, 2017). Even if houses in the Vosges mountains represented more than half of the observations, our sample remained important at this stage: 19 998 observations remained. Finally, we dropped observations with irrelevant housing characteristics (number of rooms, for example): the final sample has 10 801 observations. [SOURCE: Page_197]

### 3.2. Measuring distances to amenities

Distance to amenities were calculated using the Qgis nearest neighbour join (NNjoin) plugin, that joins two vector layers (the input and the join layer) based on nearest neighbour relationships. Distances were estimated between each land use category and the nearest houses. Distances to administrative centers (« chefs-lieux ») has also been calculated. [SOURCE: Page_197]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 4718669
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 3.3. computation of buffers
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
### 3.3. Computation of buffers

Buffers around houses were mapped using Qgis. It allows us to capture the spatial domination of land uses (Osland et al. 2022) within various radius. Several buffers were needed, because environmental amenities can have different effects at various spatial scales: Osseni et al. (2019) found that the effect of the same environmental amenity can differ at different spatial scales. In the land use context, we can imagine that some agricultural land uses cause disamenities (because of the smell of agricultural activities, for instance) that are overtaken by some positive effect at a larger scale (landscape diversification, for instance). In the literature different buffer sizes have been considered, and the number of buffers can vary: Buffers under 1km seem to be mostly used (Liljenstolpe, 2011; Kong et al. 2007; Irwin 2002), and, in particular, 500m (see Osland et al. (2022)) sometimes with other ones (Coinon et al. [SOURCE: Page_197]

195 [SOURCE: Page_197]

2023; Liljenstolpe, 2011; Kong et al. 2007). Some studies use both smaller and bigger radius than 1km (Coinon et al. 2023; Ready and Abdalla 2005). Alternatively, Cavailhès et al. (2009) tested six different radius areas to take into account the depth of the landscape viewshed: 0- 70, 70-140, 140-280, 280-1200, 1.2-6, and 6-40 km. The most important issue seems to consider different spatial scales. In this paper, we distinguish 3 different buffer sizes: 250m, 500m and 1km. It should allow us to capture very local effects as well as more extented ones. [SOURCE: Page_198]

[CHUNK_METADATA]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

### 3.4. House characteristics

We kept 5 main housing characteristics for our analysis: the constant house price, the surface (m²), the number of rooms, the number of bathrooms and the number of floors. All the housing characteristics we kept had a filling rate over 90%. On the contrary, some other variables that could have been interesting for our analysis (presence of terrace or balcony for example) were poorly filled. Constant prices before tax were obtained using the INSEE house price index^33. [SOURCE: Page_198]

### 4. Analysis of econometric results

We estimated econometric results for our different specifications: distance to environmental amenities and spatial domination of these amenities within various size buffers. We used Poisson estimation with high-dimensional fixed effects (Correia et al. 2020). Poisson regression is an alternative for estimating linear regression applied to a log-transformed dependent variable. We used the Stata command ppmlhdfe (Correia et al. 2020) to deal with multiple fixed-effects (in our case, year of transaction and municipalities). This command automatically dropped observations that are either singletons or separated by a fixed effect: keeping these observations could lead to overstate statistical significance (Correia, 2015). In our analysis, 15 observations were in that case. [SOURCE: Page_198]

House characteristics are all significant at a 1% level. The coefficient associated with the number of floors is negative. Osseni et al. (2021) found the same result, but not significant. Regarding the distances to the different land uses, results are mixed: the distance between houses and meadows is negative and quite significant, which means that closer meadows are [SOURCE: Page_198]

33 See: https://www.insee.fr/fr/statistiques/series/105071770 [SOURCE: Page_198]

196 [SOURCE: Page_198]

from the house, higher is the price. The coefficient for meadows with the square specification is nevertheless not significant. The distance to administrative centers is positive and significant, which may appear as counter-intuitive, especially in rural areas. However the square variable is negative and significant. If we take a close look at the results in the area where environmental measures take place, the distance to agriculture is positive and significant: this result may be surprising, considering the nonuse of pesticides on the impluvium. But agricultural practices can cause some disamenities (the smell or noise induced by some activities for example). One explanation can be the presence of methanisation units. If these units are located in all the Vosges^34, the presence of these units in the impluvium (in use or in construction) could overtake the positive influence of these agri-environmental measures. All the units in the department are related to farming activities (and not to water treatment plants, for instance) and a large majority of their inputs are livestock manure. Moreover, the digestate is used for spreading (ADEME, 2022). Bourdin et al. (2020) remind us that these buildings can be close to houses. They found that their development can be associated with some inconveniences: noise, smell, pollution but also risk of property depreciation. Moreover, the mistrust towards it can come from a feeling of place attachment to a territory that could be degraded, especially on a preserved area like the impluvium. [SOURCE: Page_199]

34 See: https://www.vosgesmatin.fr/economie/2019/07/20/produire-de-l-energie-a-partir-de-dechets [SOURCE: Page_199]

The second part of our results consist in testing the influence of land use spatial domination on hedonic prices. Buffer areas were designed using Qgis: we defined 3 different buffer areas perimeters around houses: 250m, 500m and 1km. Results for 250m buffers and 1km buffers are reported below. The estimation for 500m buffers is in annex 2. Coefficients for land uses variables are expressed relatively to urban areas, which is the reference here. First, the meadows and agriculture shares close to houses has a positive impact on their prices: one interpretation possible is that it offers landscape diversification and openness: It seems that these positive effects of agriculture overtake its potential negative influence (smell, noise etc.). However, these effects are not robust in the impluvium. But the forest share (with the square specification) has a positive impact on house prices in the area. We could think that forest areas participate in landscape closeness when they are so close to homes. But Cavailhès et al. (2009) found that the view of trees is valued more than their mere presence. Results about forest share can be sensitive to forest density, species etc. In our case, the mean density of forest within the 250m radius is 5.8%, which is not enough to highly contribute to landscape closeness. [SOURCE: Page_202]

Looking at the same model with 1km buffer, some results are consistent with the 250m radius. Meadows still have a positive impact on house prices, with a stronger and more significant coefficient, which means that it impacts house prices positively. This result even holds (less significantly, though) with the 500m radius (annex), which to indicate its robustness. The coefficient associated to the forest share is positive and slightly significant in a 1km radius: at that distance, the positive aspect on prices may be more linked to others forest benefits, such as recreational value of biodiversity spot. This could validate Nestlé Waters’ efforts in terms of forest management: Since 1999, a partnership has been implemented between the company and the french national forestry office (ONF) to manage its private forest. One objective of the management plan is to deal with public reception: forest landscape management is taken into account, especially next to walking paths and trails (Deck, 2008). Finally, agriculture seems to have a positive influence on prices in the impluvium: at that distance, negative impacts of agricultural activities (noise, pollution, smell etc.) are probably less important than positive ones (landscape diversification etc.). [SOURCE: Page_204]

### 5. Discussion and conclusion

This paper aimed at investigating the effect of agri-environmental measures, related to a PES scheme, on house prices. This article contributes to the literature in several ways: [SOURCE: Page_206]

First, hedonic analysis on rural territories are pretty scarces (White and Leefers, 2007; Le Goffe; 1996). Such papers may bring evidences about how crucial environmental amenities can be for rural development. Authors of recent reports (IGEDD, 2023; MASA, 2021) call for rural amenities to be valued and fairly remunerated. Rural hedonic studies can provide a scientific basis to such valuation. Besides, this article investigates PES spillovers and contributes to the literature on spillovers’ valuation of environmental policies through hedonic pricing, following Maslianskaïa-Pautrel and Baumont (2016b) and Liu et al. (2020). Such environmental spillovers could have been generated by other entities, such as natural regional parks. But our analysis is mainly based on land uses that are not specific to one particular site. Moreover, the part of the “ballon des Vosges” park that is in les Vosges is located in the mountainous area, which was excluded from our analysis. [SOURCE: Page_206]

We used CLC and Perval data to assess how agri-environmental measures could influence house prices in the Vosges. We divided land use categories in five main groups: agriculture, forest, meadows, urban area and others. In hedonic studies investigating the impact of land use on real estate prices, other categorizations exist (Osseni et al. 2021). As the way categories are constructed may impact the results, they can obviously be discussed: for example, we put “annual crops associated with permanent ones” in agriculture, which makes sense, but we could also oppose permanent crops to temporary ones in two different categories, to see which one had the bigger impact on prices. We tested the influence of land uses on prices using distance and spatial domination. For the latter, we defined buffers around houses with various radius: 250m, 500m and 1km. It is quite similar to what already exists in the literature (Liljenstolpe, 2011, Irwin, 2002), we could have done differently: For example, Coinon et al. (2023) defined buffers from 500m to 5km. Considering bigger radius could indeed be interesting, as Osseni et al. (2019) indicate that farm activities can be spatially fragmented and rarely have all their plots grouped together in a single municipality. It suggests that effects of such activities may be felt at long distances from farm buildings. But as our results are still [SOURCE: Page_206]

204 [SOURCE: Page_206]

preliminary, other buffers will maybe be considered. Distance to specific urban amenities, like schools, were for the moment, not included in the analysis: we used spatial fixed effects to control for omitted variables. In particular they are expected to capture elements that do not vary within the neighborhood (von Graevenitz and Panduro, 2015). Their use in hedonic analysis is discussed in von Graevenitz and Panduro (2015). [SOURCE: Page_207]

Our results show the positive influence of meadows on house prices. This is in line with Vanslembrouck et al. (2005) but not with Osseni et al. (2019) or with Liljenstolpe (2011). One explanation for Osseni et al. (2019) can be that permanent grasslands are mainly agricultural wetlands in Brittany. The negative effect can be associated with flood risk. For Liljenstolpe (2011) negative coefficients are associated with semi-natural grasslands and grazing land, while ours is specific to permanent grasslands. Cavailhès et al. (2009) found that crops and meadows seen between 70 and 280 m have a positive effect on house prices which is consistent with our 250m radius results. Finally, forests seem to have a positive effect considering the 1km buffer, which is consistent with Garrod and Willis (1992). Negative influence of forest can also be found (Vanslembrouck et al. 2005) but weaker feeling of closeness with this buffer could explain this result. [SOURCE: Page_207]

This study provides interesting results to discuss spillovers from PES schemes. In addition to direct environmental benefits (for water quality for instance), they have co-benefits for residents as well and may impact house prices. These co-benefits and environmental amenities in general must be taken into account in public policies and rightly valued, especially at a time where they are widely discussed at the political level^35 (IGEDD, 2023). [SOURCE: Page_207]

35 See https://www.senat.fr/amendements/2020-2021/667/Amdt_1668.html [SOURCE: Page_207]

205 [SOURCE: Page_207]

Chapitre 4 : Data production on environmental preferences for agri- environmental measures in the Vittel impluvium [SOURCE: Page_221]

219 [SOURCE: Page_221]

Data production on environmental preferences for agri-environmental measures in the Vittel impluvium [SOURCE: Page_222]

Written with Claire Montagné-Huck, Serge Garcia and Jens Abildtrup [SOURCE: Page_222]

220 [SOURCE: Page_222]

1. Presentation of data from a survey of residents' recreational preferences in the Vittel region (France) [SOURCE: Page_223]

Implementing measures to preserve the quality of spring water (controlling agricultural uses, restricting the use of pesticides, planting hedgerows, etc.) generates benefits beyond the water resource. Indeed, residents can benefit from landscapes and recreational sites shaped by these measures. Economic valuation techniques make it possible to quantify these co-benefits in monetary terms. [SOURCE: Page_223]

To this end, a survey, proposing a choice experiment exercise, was conducted, face-to-face, between November 2019 and August 2020, among over 600 households living in the vicinity of the Vittel Impluvium area. A total of 588 complete and usable responses were obtained. [SOURCE: Page_223]

The questionnaire included 75 questions, covering 1/ the socio-economic characteristics of the individuals surveyed; 2/ their sensitivity to environmental issues; 3/ the characteristics of their recreational visits; 4/ a choice experiment exercise involving hypothetical site choices for their recreational activities. [SOURCE: Page_223]

The final database includes 167 variables (numeric, categorical or alphanumeric).The anonymized data are stored in https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/ and available in the BETA collection https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/dataverse.xhtml?alias=PHD-BETA .This includes the objectives and modalities of the survey, the explanation of variables, the raw data (excel file) and the econometric treatment on stata and the other metadata; The DOI associated with this data is https://doi.org/10.57745/DSGPPR. [SOURCE: Page_223]

The purpose of this document is to present the data obtained through this work. [SOURCE: Page_223]

Since the 1980s, Nestlé-Waters, which operates the Vittel and Contrexéville drinking water springs, has been concerned about the impact of human activities on the quality of water resources. Through partnerships with the French National Institute for Agricultural Research (INRA, now INRAE), specific measures have been implemented locally to protect water quality. These measures have repercussions that go beyond preserving the quality of water resources. Indeed, impacts have been noted in terms of biodiversity, landscape quality, etc. generating benefits for local populations and tourists alike. In 2018, a PhD thesis project in economics began, with the aim of contributing to the measurement of the economic values [SOURCE: Page_223]

221 [SOURCE: Page_223]

generated by these "co-benefits". The purpose of this section is to present the initial data obtained as part of this thesis. A DCE has been proposed to assess the impacts of the water protection measures implemented, on the recreational values of residents in the Vittel-Contrex Impluvium area. [SOURCE: Page_224]

The Vittel-Contrex impluvium covers an area of over 11,400 hectares and includes 17 communes. It covers the entire watershed that supplies the springs used to bottle the various mineral waters produced in the region by Nestlé Waters (Vittel, Contrex, Hépar). [SOURCE: Page_224]

As the survey presented here was aimed at residents of the impluvium area, a perimeter with a radius of around 15 km was defined around the impluvium in order to select the communes for our sample. Within this perimeter, 13 communes were selected for the sample: we kept the 3 main communes (Vittel, Contrexéville and Mirecourt), and 10 other ones were selected randomly. The initial sample of around 600 households was constructed by selecting 5% of households living in the 3 largest communes and 10% of households living in the municipalities. Household selection was based on a random walk protocol. The household member authorized to answer the questionnaire was the last adult to have celebrated a birthday. [SOURCE: Page_224]

Figure 4.1 shows the communes included in the sample and the number of households surveyed. [SOURCE: Page_224]

Figure 4-1: Municipalities selected in the Vittel impluvium [SOURCE: Page_224]

The survey questionnaire was constructed within BETA and administered face-to-face over the period from November 19, 2019 to August 28, 2020 (with an interruption linked to the [SOURCE: Page_224]

222 [SOURCE: Page_224]

containment due to Covid-19, from spring 2020). Wood'Up (http://www.woodup- consulting.com/) was commissioned to carry out the survey in the field. [SOURCE: Page_225]

The questionnaire comprised 75 questions, divided into 4 themes: [SOURCE: Page_225]

1/ the socio-economic characteristics of the individuals surveyed ; [SOURCE: Page_225]

2/ their sensitivity to environmental issues and their attachment to the area; [SOURCE: Page_225]

3/ the characteristics of their recreational visits; [SOURCE: Page_225]

4/ a choice experiment exercise involving hypothetical site choices for their recreational activities. [SOURCE: Page_225]

Of the 624 interviews conducted, 588 were complete and usable for our analysis. [SOURCE: Page_225]

People who had declared that they had visited a forest or countryside site in the past year or in the past 5 years (N=380) were offered 8 sequences of choices between the last site they had visited and 2 alternatives, described synthetically on the basis of 8 attributes. [SOURCE: Page_225]

The attributes used to describe the proposed sites have been discussed during a focus group with residents of the study area. Attributes included the presence of tourist facilities, the presence of hiking trails, the presence of water features (lakes, rivers, etc.), the use of pesticides, different agricultural land uses, the presence of hedges and biodiversity, the variety of tree species present and the distance to the site (Table 4.1). [SOURCE: Page_225]

The resulting database represents an original source of information, unavailable elsewhere. These data were used to estimate residents' willingness to pay for the attributes of recreational sites in the forest or countryside. The results of this work are available in Amiri et al. (2021) and Amiri et al. (2022a). [SOURCE: Page_226]

These results can be used to support resource managers and public decision-makers when implementing recreational developments or measures to preserve natural areas. The availability of anonymized data will enable them to be reused in the scientific field to replicate the results of Amiri et al. (2021) and Amiri et al. (2022a) or to be integrated into comparisons of similar case studies or meta-analyses. [SOURCE: Page_226]

The anonymized data are stored in https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/ and available in the BETA collection https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/dataverse.xhtml?alias=PHD- BETA .This includes the objectives and modalities of the survey, the explanation of variables, the raw data (excel file) and the econometric treatment on stata and the other metadata; The DOI associated with this data is https://doi.org/10.57745/DSGPPR. [SOURCE: Page_226]

228 [SOURCE: Page_230]

2. Presentation of data from a survey on the estimation of non-use values associated with the implementation of water quality and biodiversity protection measures in the Rupt de Mad watershed (France). [SOURCE: Page_231]

This section describes a survey carried out as part of a partnership project between INRAE and Nestlé Waters. The overall aim of the project was to have a better understanding of people's preferences and willingness to pay for the implementation of water quality protection measures and for the co-benefits generated by these measures (protection of biodiversity, agricultural and forest landscapes, etc.). [SOURCE: Page_231]

The survey presented here specifically aims at assessing the NUV associated with such measures. It therefore focuses on a public that does not derive any direct benefit from the protected resource through actual, immediate or future use. [SOURCE: Page_231]

The study site, the Rupt de Mad watershed, is located in eastern France. The targeted individuals for the survey (carried out online between June 28, 2021 and July 30, 2021) are spread across France, with a particular focus on inhabitants of the Rhine-Meuse basin. [SOURCE: Page_231]

The sample was built according to age, gender and socio-professional category quotas. A total of 977 usable responses were obtained. [SOURCE: Page_231]

The questionnaire included a set of 29 questions relating to: 1/ respondents' environmental sensitivity (in terms of product consumption habits or global environmental issues); 2/ the economic valuation of respondents' preferences for the implementation of programs aiming at the renaturation of watercourses, the promotion of forestry and agricultural management practices favorable to water quality and rural landscapes (through an increase in drinking water bills); 3/ respondents' socio-economic characteristics. The final database includes 110 variables   (numeric,    categorical    or   alphanumeric).     The    data   are   stored    in https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/, together with the questionnaires, the completed data and a summary report. [SOURCE: Page_231]

The data presented in this document are part of a partnership between Nestlé Waters and the French national research institute for agriculture, food and the environment INRAE (formerly INRA), represented by the bureau of theoretical and applied economics (BETA). The overall aim of the project was to shed an economic light on the environmental and social benefits co- produced by actions to improve water quality. In particular, the aim is to assess the well-being [SOURCE: Page_231]

229 [SOURCE: Page_231]

that society derives from actions taken to improve water quality and preserve the natural environment. This work is based on three field surveys carried out with a variety of audiences. [SOURCE: Page_232]

The first one (see Amiri et al. 2022a) focused on assessing the values that residents derive from the preservation of water quality and landscapes in the Vittel impluvium. The second, which we present here, aims at analyzing the NUV associated with these measures. The third survey will examine the values generated by these measures for tourists and non- residents in the Vittel region. [SOURCE: Page_232]

NUV are economic values associated with the preservation or enhancement of a resource, without the person assigning the value being linked to the resource by any present or future possibility of using it. The notion of non-use value is therefore based on the idea that we can derive a well-being, a satisfaction, measurable in monetary terms, that is simply linked to the acceptability of a better use of the resource by others than ourselves, or simply for the improvement of the resource itself. [SOURCE: Page_232]

In order to estimate such non-use values in the context of setting up programs to preserve rural landscapes and water resources, a survey was carried out throughout mainland France. [SOURCE: Page_232]

The study focused on the Rupt de Mad watershed, a sparsely populated area of around 385 km² at the crossroads of the Meuse, Moselle and Meurthe et Moselle departments. This watershed supplies drinking water to nearly 200,000 inhabitants of the Metz metropolitan area. [SOURCE: Page_232]

The survey questionnaire was designed at BETA and administered online between June 28, 2021 and July 30, 2021, by the Kantar compagny (https://www.kantar.com/fr). The sample was designed to be representative in terms of respondents' age, gender and socio-professional category. [SOURCE: Page_232]

In terms of location, we included in the sample respondents living at different distances from the study area, to be able to test the effect of distance from the respondent's site on his willingness to pay and identify NUV. Indeed, people who live a long way from the study site do not, a priori, attribute any value linked to a possible use of the resource. The values elicited for them must therefore represent "pure" NUV. [SOURCE: Page_232]

A total of 1005 questionnaires were completed in 654 communes across France (see figure 4.2). [SOURCE: Page_232]

230 [SOURCE: Page_232]

Figure 4-2: Distribution of respondents over the French territory [SOURCE: Page_233]

The survey questionnaire consisted of 3 parts, related to: [SOURCE: Page_233]

- the socio-economic characteristics of respondents; [SOURCE: Page_233]

- respondents' environmental awareness (consumption habits and sensitivity to global environmental issues) [SOURCE: Page_233]

- the economic valuation of values linked to the implementation of measures to preserve water quality and agricultural and forest landscapes. Implementation of a choice experiment technique to estimate willingness to pay for different attributes through an increase in household water bills. [SOURCE: Page_233]

Questions relating to the comprehension of this last part of the questionnaire enabled us to identify individuals who had not understood the approach, and to exclude them from the final sample. We therefore have 977 fully-completed and usable questionnaires for analysis. [SOURCE: Page_233]

The resulting database provides original information not available elsewhere. These data have enabled us to assess respondents' willingness to pay for the attributes proposed in the choice 231 [SOURCE: Page_233]

experiment: pesticide use, renaturation of watercourses, type of fertilizer used, plantation of hedgerows, forest management methods (Amiri et al. 2022a; 2023a). This information can help resource managers and public decision-makers to better understand the incentive mechanisms needed to finance such actions. The data can be reused in the scientific field for replication of the results of this study, as well as for inclusion in comparisons with similar case studies or meta-analyses. [SOURCE: Page_234]

The anonymized data are stored in https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/ and available in the BETA collection https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/dataverse.xhtml?alias=PHD- BETA .This includes the objectives and modalities of the survey, the explanation of variables, the raw data (excel file) and the econometric treatment on stata and the other metadata; The DOI associated with this data is https://doi.org/10.57745/MXDUB$_2$. [SOURCE: Page_234]

3. Presentation of survey data on tourist preferences for forest and agricultural landscape management and impact on the local economy in the Vittel region (France) [SOURCE: Page_238]

This section provides the description of a survey targeting tourists in the Vittel impluvium in the Vosges department (France) carried out among people present in the communes of Vittel and Contrexéville during the 2023 summer (from July 17 to September 12, 2023). [SOURCE: Page_238]

The survey was based on a structured questionnaire, administered face-to-face to 479 individuals, mainly in the Vittel thermal park and the Contrexéville tourist office. [SOURCE: Page_238]

The information gathered covered people who visited the area alone, as a couple, as a family or in a group. They included data on the motivations for the visit (price, duration of transport, thalasso, nature, reputation) and its characteristics (duration, expenditure), and on the characteristics of the respondents (region of origin, gender, qualifications, jobs, income, consumption of bottled water). [SOURCE: Page_238]

The data also contain results on whether or not tourists would accept the implementation of an environmental tax to help finance actions to improve water quality and biodiversity (absence of pesticides, planting of hedges, renaturation of watercourses, etc.). We first measure the acceptance of an additional tax per person on the bill for a restaurant lunch (for a one-day visit) or on the bill for a hotel night (for a longer visit), and then, their maximum willingness to pay. Data were collected using LimeSurvey software. [SOURCE: Page_238]

The data set contains a total of 223 numeric, categorical and non-numeric variables. The data are stored in https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/ along with the questionnaires, completed data and a summary report. [SOURCE: Page_238]

In addition to preserving water quality, the actions undertaken by Agrivair to maintain and even improve environmental conditions favorable to a good quality of raw water resources, particularly at Nestlé Waters' water production sites, also have other beneficial environmental, social and economic effects. In particular, the practices implemented protect biodiversity, modify landscapes and can even have an impact on economic areas such as tourism and the local economy in general. Doing so, they affect the well-being of populations. [SOURCE: Page_238]

236 [SOURCE: Page_238]

We present here data from a survey conducted in summer 2023 (July 17 to September 12, 2023), among tourists identified as being present for leisure activities on the Vittel impluvium, more specifically the communes of Vittel and Contrexéville, in the Vosges department (France). This survey was designed to study the effect of the measures on the number of tourists visiting the hotels, vacation clubs, campsites and thermal baths in Vittel and Contrexéville because of the quality of their water. In particular, this survey seeks to identify spending in local shops and crafts, but also to assess their willingness to pay (WTP) for a stay in an improved local environment as a result of these actions to protect water quality. [SOURCE: Page_239]

The questionnaire was implemented using the LimeSurvey software. A copy of the questionnaire is available in the appendix, showing the structure of the questionnaire, the questions and proposed answers, and the logic for administering the questions and sub- questions. [SOURCE: Page_239]

To encourage tourists to complete the questionnaire, it was mentioned that a bottle of Château Couhins wine (Pessac Léognan appellation - operated by INRAE) and a meal for two in a gourmet restaurant would be drawn at the end of the survey period. [SOURCE: Page_239]

A total of 506 people were interviewed, mainly face-to-face (479)36, to find out their preferences in terms of management of agricultural and forest landscapes, and to estimate their CAP for implementing measures to preserve biodiversity and water quality. Of the 506 interviews carried out, 19 were not completed and were excluded from the sample, as they could not be used. We also withdrew the responses of 12 residents and professionals who did not fit into the target audience of our survey. Our final sample therefore includes 475 usable responses. [SOURCE: Page_239]

In addition to standard questions relating to respondents' socio-economic characteristics, we asked people about the characteristics of their visit to the region, their bottled water consumption practices and their knowledge of water quality and biodiversity preservation measures That take place in the region. A contingent valuation was also proposed to estimate the WTP for implementing additional measures to preserve biodiversity and water quality. The proposed means of payment was a tax designed to help finance actions to protect water quality and biodiversity. The amounts proposed ranged from 0.5€ to 10€ for a tax deducted [SOURCE: Page_239]

36 The survey was conducted face-to-face with a single interviewer on a tablet. Number of interviewers, locations mainly in the Vittel spa park and the Contrexéville tourist office. Some paper questionnaires were also made available to passers-by at the Vittel and Contrexéville tourist offices. [SOURCE: Page_239]

237 [SOURCE: Page_239]

from the bill for a restaurant lunch (for an all-day visit) and from 0.5€ to 5€/night/person for an increase in the tourist tax (if visits lasted several days). [SOURCE: Page_240]

Our database provides original information not available elsewhere. These data will enable us to assess the preferences and estimate the WTP of tourists in the Vittel and Contrexéville region for the implementation of measures to preserve water quality and biodiversity. This information can help resource managers and public decision-makers to better understand the incentive mechanisms to be put in place to finance such actions. The data can be reused in the scientific field to replicate the results of this study, as well as to be integrated into comparisons with similar case studies or meta-analyses. [SOURCE: Page_240]

The anonymized data are stored in https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/ and available in the BETA collection https://entrepot.recherche.data.gouv.fr/dataverse.xhtml?alias=PHD- BETA .This includes the objectives and modalities of the survey, the explanation of variables, the raw data (excel file) and the econometric treatment on stata and the other metadata; The DOI associated with this data is https://doi.org/10.57745/SMMQXT. [SOURCE: Page_240]

Tables 4.9 to 4.12 give some characteristics of the sample, respondents, their visits and knowledge of environmental protection measures in place. [SOURCE: Page_240]

### 4. Annex

Questionnaire quatrième chapitre Bonjour, [SOURCE: Page_251]

Nous vous remercions d'avoir accepté de répondre à ce questionnaire, qui a pour cadre un projet de recherche INRAE (Institut National de Recherche pour l'Agriculture, l'Alimentation et l'Environnement), dans lequel nous travaillons sur la région de Vittel pour étudier les préférences en termes de gestion des paysages forestiers et agricoles. Il n'y a pas de bonne ou de mauvaise réponse, mais il est important que vous répondiez à toutes les questions. Vos réponses seront strictement anonymes 2. Une bouteille de vin Château Couhins (appellation : Pessac-Léognan), exploité par INRAE est à gagner ! Le gagnant sera désigné par tirage au sort sous réserve de nous communiquer vos coordonnées email ou postales à la fin du questionnaire. [SOURCE: Page_251]

1) Ce questionnaire vous a-t-il été distribué à ? a) La station thermale de Vittel b) Vit tel ta nature c) l’hôtel Cosmos [SOURCE: Page_251]

2) Vous venez dans la région : Veuillez sélectionner une réponse ci-dessous: a) A des fins de loisirs b) Pour des raisons professionnelles c) Je suis résident Si réponse a) à la question 2 [SOURCE: Page_251]

3) Où habitez-vous ? (Indiquez le nom de la commune et le code postal si vous vivez en France, et le pays si vous vivez ailleurs). [SOURCE: Page_251]

Commune : Code postal : Hors France, pays : [SOURCE: Page_251]

2 Les informations recueillies serviront uniquement à ce projet de recherche et respectent les principes du règlement général sur la protection des données (RGPD). Si vous ne souhaitez pas que vos réponses soient utilisées par la suite, faites-le savoir à l’adresse suivante : [EMAIL_REDACTED] 249 [SOURCE: Page_251]

4) Vous êtes venu(e) : [SOURCE: Page_252]

Veuillez sélectionner une réponse ci-dessous: a) seul(e) b) en couple c) en famille d) en groupe préciser le nombre de personnes : [SOURCE: Page_252]

5) Indiquez, selon vous, l’importance de chacun des critères suivants dans le choix de Vittel et de sa région comme destination touristique. [SOURCE: Page_252]

Pas          Peu          Assez      Important     Très      Extrêmement important    important    important                important    important Le prix du séjour Le temps de trajet L’environnement naturel (paysage, contact avec la nature etc.) Les activités à effectuer La notoriété du site [SOURCE: Page_252]

6) Quelle est la durée de votre séjour à Vittel ou dans les environs ? [SOURCE: Page_252]

Veuillez sélectionner une réponse ci-dessous : a) Moins d’une journée (je fais l’aller-retour dans la journée) b) Plus d’une journée (indiquer le nombre de jours dans le cadre suivant) : [SOURCE: Page_252]

7) Consommez-vous de l'eau en bouteille ? [SOURCE: Page_252]

Veuillez sélectionner une réponse ci-dessous a) Je ne consomme jamais d'eau en bouteille b) Je consomme occasionnellement de l'eau en bouteille c) Je consomme majoritairement de l'eau en bouteille d) Je consomme uniquement de l'eau en bouteille 250 [SOURCE: Page_252]

Si réponse b), c) ou d) à la question 7 [SOURCE: Page_253]

8) Vous avez répondu que vous consommiez de l'eau en bouteille. Consommez-vous l'eau d'une ou plusieurs des marques suivantes ? [SOURCE: Page_253]

Cochez-la ou les réponses a) Marque de distributeur b) Vittel c) Contrex d) Cristaline e) Evian f) Hépar g) Volvic h) Aucune de ces marques [SOURCE: Page_253]

9) Le groupe Nestlé, propriétaire de la marque Vittel© exploite les sources d'eau situées sur le bassin versant de Vittel. Pour faire face aux risques de pollution de l'eau, le groupe a décidé de mettre en place des partenariats avec les agriculteurs locaux afin de préserver la qualité de l’eau (arrêt d'utilisation de pesticides sur le site, arrêt de la culture du maïs, etc.). En parallèle, le groupe mène des actions en faveur de la biodiversité (plantations de haies, renaturation de cours d'eau, etc.). Indirectement, ces mesures, qui sont financées par Nestlé, profitent aux habitants et aux touristes (à travers la diversification du paysage, par exemple). Aviez-vous connaissance de telles actions ? [SOURCE: Page_253]

Veuillez sélectionner une réponse ci-dessous a) Je n’avais pas connaissance de ces actions b) J’avais connaissance des actions menées pour améliorer et préserver de la qualité de l’eau c) J’avais connaissance des actions menées en faveur de la biodiversité d) J’avais connaissance des actions menées pour la qualité de l’eau et la biodiversité [SOURCE: Page_253]

Si réponse b), c) ou d) à la question précédente [SOURCE: Page_253]

10) Ces actions de préservation de l’environnement ont-elles joué un rôle sur votre décision de vous rendre dans la région ? [SOURCE: Page_253]

Veuillez sélectionner une réponse ci-dessous : [SOURCE: Page_253]

251 [SOURCE: Page_253]

a) Oui b) Non [SOURCE: Page_254]
